one  was  formed  with  the  western.

Q.  Of  what  nature  do  you  consider  the  patri-
archal dignity  and  power  to  have  been  ?

A.  We  think  it  to  have  been  of  human,  not
divine  or  apostolic  authority,  and  that  whether
we  consider  the  occasion  of  its  origin,  or  the
reason  of  its  existence,  it  must  be  to  a  great
extent  obsolete  and  impracticable.  The  apostles
indeed,  in  the  first  propagation  of  the  gospel,
seem  to  have  followed  the  civil  form  of  govern-
ment ;  as  each  city  of  importance  in  the  empire
had  its  senate  and  president,  so  each  Church  had
its  presbyters  and  bishop.  According  to  this
model  St.  Paul  directs  Titus  to  ordain  elders  in
Crete  *  in  every  city.  The  division  of  the  Roman
empire  into  provinces  was  coeval  with  Christian-
ity, and  each  province  was  under  a  Praetor  or
Proconsul ;  about  the  time  of  Constantine  these
provinces  were   consolidated  into   dioceses,  the

*  Kara  rroXiv.


165

governors  of  which,  residing  in  the  metropolis,
were  called  eparchs  or  vicars.  The  Church,
following  the  civil  government,  had  in  each
province  a  metropolitan,  and  in  each  diocese  a
patriarch.  The  patriarchal  authority,  therefore,
was  geographical,  and  arose  out  of  the  political
state  of  the  world  at  that  time.  When  the  em-
pire ceased,  and  Europe  was  formed  into  separate
and  independent  kingdoms,  the  patriarchal  and
geographical  authority  of  the  patriarchs  ceased
also,  and  the  Church  in  all  reason  ought  again
to  conform  in  its  external  form  to  the  civil
o'overnments.  As  the  Russian  Church  is  now
independent  of  the  patriarch  of  Constantinople,
so  all  the  independent  nations  of  Europe,  have  a
right  to  their  own  national  Christianity.  That
which  is  predicated  and  justly  of  bishops,  can
never  be  affirmed  of  patriarchs,  that  they  derive
their  authority  from  the  sacred  scriptures,  the  most
certain  tradition,  the  testimony  of  all  the  Fathers,
the  decrees  of  all  councils,  and  the  practice  of  the
Church  uninterrupted.  The  patriarchal  power  of
the  bishop  of  Rome  was  constantly  repudiated
by  the  African  Churches,  and  those  of  Britain
and  Milan,  and  it  is  probable  that  the  patriar-
chal authority  of  the  bishop  of  Rome,  properly
speaking,  extended  no  farther  than  the  civil
authority  of  the  civil  vicar  of  the  city,  which  was
the  suburbical  Churches  of  the  ten  provinces.
Dupin,  a  Romanist,  exempts  on  the  best  histor-

m3


166

ical  evidence,  Germany,  Spain,  France,  Britain,
Africa,  Illyricum,  and  seven  Italian  provinces,
from  the  jm'isdiction  of  the  Pope  in  the  primitive
ages.  The  spiritual  monarchists  are  angry  with
this  assertion,  but  they  have  never  been  able  to
refiite  it,  and  if  they  had,  we  should  never
be  convinced  or  cajoled  to  believe  that  a  power
raised  on  a  political  or  geographical  basis  which
no  longer  exists,  is  binding  on  the  independent
nations  of  Christendom.

Q.  What  is  the  dignity  and  authority  assumed
by  the  popes  *?

A.  It  is  difficult  to  describe  the  authority  and
jurisdiction  claimed  by  the  popes,  even  according
to  the  authentic  documents  of  the  Roman  Cath-
olic Church.  It  certainly  was  the  opinion  of
Baronius,  Bellarmine,  the  council  of  Lateran,  and
the  council  of  Trent,  so  far  at  least  as  it  sanc-
tioned the  other  councils  of  the  Church,  that  the
bishop  of  Rome  had  a  supremacy  in  temporals
as  well  as  spirituals.  It  is  certain  that  the
popes  affected  to  exercise  this  universal  monar-
chy over  many  kings,  princes,    and  kingdoms.*

*  The  following  is  an  extract  from  the  Bull  of  Pope  Pius  v.
against  Queen  Elizabeth,  "  He  that  reigneth  on  high,  to  whom  is
given  all  power  in  heaven  and  earth,  hath  committed  the  one
Holy  Catholic  and  Apostolical  Church,  out  of  which  there  is  no
salvation,  to  one  alone  on  earth,  namely  to  Peter,  prince  of  the
Apostles,  and  to  the  Roman  Pontiff,  successor  of  Peter,  to  be
governed  xoith  a  plenitude  of  power :  This  one  he  hath  consti-
tuted prince  over  all  nations,  and  all  kingdoms,  that  he  might


167

The  assumption  of  such  a  power  is  so  impudent,
on  the  confession  of  its  ablest  advocates,  so
novel,  and  by  the  judgment  of  many  Roman
Catholics,  so  entirely  condemned,  that  the  tem-
poral jurisdiction  of  the  pope  is  not  now  directly
and  openly  asserted  by  the  popes  or  their  advo-
cates. It  has  not,  however,  been  authoritatively
renounced,  and  it  is  to  be  feared  that  there  are
many,  especially  among  the  Jesuits,  who  hold  the
universal  supremacy  of  the  pope  in  its  extreme
sense,  though  they  are  ashamed  or  afraid  to  own
it.  The  universal  spiritual  supremacy,  is,  how-
ever, acknowledged  by  all  Catholics,  though  in
different  senses,  and  with  different  limitations.
The  French  Church,  the  doctors  of  Sorbonne,
and  some  German  Catholics,  have  held  the
pope's  supremacy  very  lightly,and  the  popes  from
necessity,  have  tolerated  the  liberties  of  the
Oallican  and  other  Churches  ;  though  they  have
never  acknowledged  their  principles.  These
Catholics  have  been  looked  upon  as  mongrels
and  semi-romanists,  and  the  true  doctrines  of  the
Church,  court,  and  popes  of  Rome,  is,  the  pope's

pluck  up,  destroy,  dissipate,  ruinate,  plant,  and  build."  And
again,  "  He  thereby  deprives  the  Queen  of  her  pretended  right
to  the  Kingdom,  and  of  all  dominion,  dignity,  and  privileo-e  what-
soever ;  and  absolves  all  the  nobles,  subjects,  and  people  of  the
kingdom,  and  whoever  else  have  sworn  to  her,  from  their  oath,
and  all  duty  whatsoever,  in  regard  of  dominion,  fidelity,  and
obedience."  Barrow  on  the  Pope's  Supremacy,  from  which  treatise
the  few  facts  relating  to  this  point  are  chiefly  selected.


168

absolute  sovereignty,  that  he  is  the  universal
pastor  of  all  Christians  by  God's  appointment,
indefectibly  and  unalterably.  The  authority  of
bishops  is  referred  to  scripture,  history,  councils,
and  practice  uninterrupted,  but  the  supremacy
or  universal  primacy  of  the  popes  rests  upon
peculiar  grounds,  and  indeed  abstractions.  The
argument  for  it  has  been  thus  stated.  That  St.
Peter  had  a  primacy  over  the  apostles,  that  this
primacy,  with  its  prerogatives,  was  derivable  to
his  successors  the  bishops  of  Rome — that  St.  Peter
was  bishop  of  Rome  and  continued  so  till  his
death — That  the  bishops  of  Rome,  by  original
right  derived  from  him,  have  an  universal  supre-
macy and  jurisdiction  over  the  Christian  Church,
that  they  have  always  enjoyed  and  exercised
this  sovereign  power,  and  that  that  power  is  in-
defectible.

Q.  What  do  we  think  of  this  universal  supre-
macy and  spiritual  monarchy  assumed  by  the
popes  and  acknowledged  by  his  adherents  ?

A.  We  think  it  an  arrogant  impiety  ;  the  most
impudent  and  indefensible  fraud  that  ever  was
practiced  on  the  world,  and  that  it  has  been  "  the
pest  of  Christendom."  With  respect  to  St.  Peter,
we  admit  that  he  was  probably  the  oldest,  and
the  first  ordained,  or  rather  called,  of  the  apos-
tles ;  that  he  had  great  worth  and  excellence ;
that  his  confession  and  conduct  showed  his  sin-
cerity ;  that  his  age,    experience,   and  superior


169

talents,  gave  him  a  precedency  in  action  on
many  occasions  that  was  perfectly  natural,  but
that  though  in  many  respects  on  these  accounts
he  appeared  to  take  the  lead  in  the  early  pro-
ceedings of  the  Church,  he  had  no  superiority
defined,  and  no  personal  prerogative  given  above
the  other  Apostles.  On  the  contrary  that  the
idea  of  such  a  superiority  and  personal  primacy
is  excluded  by  the  few  following,  amongst  a  host
of  facts  and  reasons,  in  bar  of  such  an  authority.
Such  an  authority  could  not  be  claimed,  but  by
an  express  commission,  granted  in  terms  which
admit  of  no  doubt,  and  which  are  so  clearly  re-
vealed that  the  world  may  understand  them.  It
must  be  as  clearly  revealed  that  St.  Peter  was
appointed  primate  and  monarch  of  the  Apostles,
as  that  our  Lord  appointed  twelve  Apostles,
and  it  must  as  clearly  appear  in  the  acts  of  the
Apostles,  that  St.  Peter  claimed,  and  that  the
Apostles  acknowledged  this  superiority,  as  that
the  Apostles  asserted  their  apostolic  authority.
But  nowhere  in  the  evangelical  or  apostolic  his-
tory, can  we  find  this  institution,  or  the  current
recognition  of  it,  as  we  do  the  institution  and
authority  of  the  Apostles.  We  find  that  during
the  life-time  of  our  Lord,  and  even  at  the  last
supper,  that  the  twelve  disputed  which  should
be  the  greatest,  which  they  would  hardly  have
done  if  Christ  had  already  determined  that  Peter
was  their  prince  and  king.     When  our  Lord  de-


170

cided  their  question,  he  did  Dot  determine  Peter
or  any  other  to  be  the  chief,  he  pronounced  them
equal ;  he  placed  a  little  child  in  the  midst  of
them,  and  told  them  that  whoever  should  in
innocence,  simplicity,  and  submission,  most  re-
semble that  infant,  should  be  morally  the  great-
est. St.  Luke  tells  us,  that  after  a  most  patient
enquiry  among  the  Apostles,  he  had  acquired  a
perfect  understanding  of  all  things  of  importance
believed  among  Christians,  and  he  wrote  his
book  of  the  acts  of  the  Apostles,  to  explain  the
manner  in  which  the  Apostles  at  the  first  execu-
ted their  commission ;  but  neither  in  the  gospel,
nor  in  the  acts  of  the  Apostles,  do  we  find  a
single  word  of  Peter's  supremacy.  The  scrip-
tures on  which  the  Romanists  found  their  impos-
ture, were  not  so  understood,  asserted,  explained,
or  alluded  to,  by  Peter  or  any  other  Apostle.
What  Apostle  ever  understood  that  by  the  me-
taphor of  a  rock,  was  intend  ed  an  universal  bishop?
When  did  St.  Peter  so  understand  it  ?  What  man
of  common  sense  can  so  understand  it  ?  Of  all
the  thousand  interpretations,  which  have  been
given  of  that  metaphor,  where  is  one  of  equal  ab-
surdity with  this  to  be  found  ?  If  the  power  of
the  keys  gave  to  St.  Peter  an  independent  and
plenary  authority  as  Apostle  to  found  and  estab-
lish christian  churches,  that  same  authority  had
Paul  and  all  the  other  Apostles.  So  far  as  that
authority,  in  a  subordinate  sense   is  derivative,


171

so  far  as  it  is  proper  and  reasonable,  it  is  exer-
cised not  only  by  the  bishop  of  Rome,  but  by
every  church  in  which  the  word  and  sacraments
are  rightly  administered,  and  which  exercises  a
christian  discipline  over  its  members.  Who  in  his
senses  can  believe  that  by  the  power  of  the  keys
was  meant  the  papal  tyranny  ?  What  scripture,
what  early  fathers,  councils  or  synods  teach  us  to
believe  that  those  keys  are  exclusively  deposited
with  the  bishop  of  Rome  ?  It  is  indeed  asserted
by  the  Papists,  that  St.  Peter  left  them  by  will,
but  that  testament  has  never  been  found,  no
certificate  of  it  has  ever  been  proved.  What
Apostle,  Father,  Council,  what  man  of  common
sense,  that  is  not  deluded  or  wishes  to  cajole
others,  ever  imagined  that  our  Lord's  admonition
to  St.  Peter  after  his  fall  "Feed  my  sheep,"  "  feed
my  lambs,"  conveyed  to  him  (any  more  than  his
look  did  when  Peter  denied  him)  a  supremacy
over  the  other  Apostles,  and  to  his  successors  a
supreme  monarchy  over  the  church  ?  St.  Peter's
epistles  indicate  a  modesty,  affection,  and  sym-
pathy with  human  infirmities  that  are  in  perfect
accordance  with  our  Lord's  parting  injunction,
but  who  ever  found  the  pope's  supremacy  in
those  epistles,  in  which  less  authority  is  assumed
than  in  any  other  inspired  document  *?  St.  Peter
does  assert  the  king's  supremacy  "  under  God"
"  Fear  God  honour  the  king;"  but  there  is
not  a  word  of  his  own  supremacy,  or  his  succes-


172

sors  for  ever.  St.  Paul's  assertion  of  his  inde-
pendence and  equality  is  also  as  clear  a  proof  as
can  be  wished  that  no  such  superiority  or  infallible
jurisdiction  existed  and  this  indeed  is  confessed  by
the  most  learned  advocate  of  the  papacy  to  be
"  a  history  hard  to  be  understood,  a  stone  of
offence,  a  rock  of  scandal,  a  rugged  place  which
St.  Austin  himself,  under  favour  could  not  pass
over  without  stumbling."

St.  Peter  therefore  had  no  primacy,  but  admit-
ting for  the  sake  of  argument,  that  he  had,
whatever  it  was,  it  was  personal.  St.  Peter  was
an  eye-witness  of  all  our  Lord  did  or  suffered — he
had  a  plenary  inspiration,  and  miraculous  power
— he  could  instruct  the  nations,  and  govern  the
Churches;  but  these  were  powers  granted  by  a
special  commission  for  the  establishment  of  the
christian  Church,  and  such  as  no  one  now
can  assert  without  imposture  and  hypocrisy.
With  respect  to  the  assertion,  that  St.  Peter
was  bishop  of  Rome  (which,  if  true,  would
not  amount  to  much )  as  it  has  never  been
proved,  so  it  may  be  fairly  denied,  and  for  these
among  other  reasons.  He  was  an  Apostle  and
fulfilled  the  genet'al  duties  of  an  Apostle,  and
therefore  it  was  neither  necessary,  nor  probable
that  he  should  assume  the  stated,  local  and  infe-
rior office  of  bishop.  His  particular  designation
to  be  the  Apostle  of  the  circumcision,  would  not
harmonize  with  such  an  appointment,  especially


173

as  the  emperor  had  at  that  time  banished  all  the
Jews  from  Rome.  If  he  were  bishop  of  Rome,
he  must  have  been  non-resident,  for  all  the  histo-
ries we  have  of  him,  represent  him  as  performing
the  duties  of  an  Apostle,  in  distant  and  various
countries.  He  must  have  been  frequently  absent
from  Rome,  for  St  Paul  in  his  epistles  to  the  Ro-
mans, and  in  his  epistles  written  from  Rome,
makes  no  particular  mention  of  St.  Peter.  That
St.  Peter  was  at  Rome  as  well  as  St.  Paul,  it  is
not  necessary  to  deny,  but  who  made  him  bishop
there,  or  whether  he  appointed  himself  no  one
knows.  The  fact,  is,  however,  that  he  appointed
other  bishops,  Linus  and  Clement  at  Rome  as  he
did  St.  Mark  at  Alexandria,  and  others.  If  all
these  things,  however,  were  true  of  St.  Peter ;  if
his  primacy  among  the  Apostles,  and  his  episco-
pacy at  Rome,  were  matters  of  certainty,  how
does  the  divine  institution  of  an  universal  bishop
and  the  indefectibility  of  the  pope's  authority
follow  *?  Is  it  not  strange,  that  such  an  impor-
tant fact  is  unattested  by  scripture  ?  that  practice
for  centuries,  that  ancient  creeds,  councils,  and
synods,  that  heretics,  pagan  historians,  kings  and
emperors,  who  might  have  been  jealous;  that  all
the  ancient  writers  mostly  relied  upon  by  the
Romanists ;  the  apostolical  canons ;  Ignatius,
Cyprian,  and  many  popes  themselves,  are  posi-
tively and  absolutely  silent  respecting  the  supre-
macy ?  In  all  the  writings  of  the  ancient  fathers,


174

who  discuss  the  most  trivial  matters,  this  is  un-
defined and  overlooked.  For  a  long  period  the
christian  Church  was  governed  by  patriarchs,
whose  authority  was  equal  and  independent,  and
during  that  time,  the  popes  themselves  protested
against  the  title  and  authority  of  an  universal
bishop.  Surely  these  facts  are  decisive  against
any,  much  less  an  universal  and  indefectible,  su-
premacy in  the  bishop  of  Rome.  The  pretended
succession  has  often  failed,  and  been  extremely
doubtful.  For  seventy  years  the  popes  were
popes  of  Avignon  and  not  of  Rome  ;  and  no  more
bishops  of  Rome  than  the  bishop  of  London  is
bishop  of  Jerusalem.  During  the  invasion  of  the
Goths,  there  were  no  bishops  of  Rome  ;  at  va-
rious times  there  have  been  heretics,  atheists,
and  two  or  three  usurpers  at  the  same  time.  These
things  might  not  in  any  degree  affect  the  general
doctrine  of  an  apostolical  episcopacy,  but  they
are  absolutely  destructive  of  an  indefectible  uni-
versal monarchy  over  the  christian  Church  exer-
cised by  the  bishop  of  Rome.

Q.  What  is  the  doctrine  of  infallibility,  asser-
ted by  the  Church  of  Rome  ?

A.  The  Romanists  dispute  among  themselves
whether  the  infallibility  is  vested  in  the  pope,  or
in  the  Church.  The  Jesuits  maintain  that  the
pope  is  infallible,  that  he  is  the  only  source  of
that  universal  and  unlimited  power  which  Christ
has  granted  to  his  Church,  that  the  Bishops  and


175

subordinate  ministers  derive  from  him  alone  their
authority  and  jurisdiction,  that  he  is  the  supreme
lawffiver  of  the  Church,  and  that  his  edicts  and
commands  cannot  be  disobeyed  without  grievous
sin.  On  the  other  hand,  there  are  Romanists
that  maintain  that  the  pope  is  liable  to  error —
that  his  authority  is  inferior  to  that  of  general
councils — that  he  is  bound  to  obey  councils
which  represent  the  Church — that  councils  may
deprive  him  of  the  papal  chair,  and  that  in  conse-
quence of  these  principles,  the  bishops  derive
their  authority  not  from  the  Roman  pontiff,  but
from  Christ  himself.  Such  contradictions  are  a
sufficient  reply  to  the  barbarous  doctrine  of  in-
fallibility. But  there  are  other  contradictions.
The  Romanists  dispute  all  the  doctrines  of  grace
and  have  maintained  the  opposite  extremes  of
Arminianism  and  Calvanism.  No  disputants
have  subtleized  religious  controversies  as  they
have  done.  They  dispute  to  opus  operatum  of
the  sacraments,  and  especially  of  penance  and
the  eucharist — the  extent  and  prerogatives  of  the
Church  itself — the  doctrines  of  morality,  and
rules  of  practice.  They  differ  also  about  the
question  of  instruction,  some  maintaining  that
children  are  to  be  instructed  in  the  doctrines  and
duties  of  the  Christian  religion,  and  others  that
a  devout  ignorance,  and  blind  and  implicit  obe-
dience to  the  Church,  are  quite  sufficient.  Vain
therefore  and  foolish,  is  the  argument  of  Bossuet


176

against  protestants,  derived  from  the  variety
of  their  opinions  and  confessions  of  faith.  His
own  exposition  of  the  Roman  Catholic  faith
was  condemned  by  one  pope  and  approved  by
another — censured  by  the  Sorbonne  in  one  cen-
tury, and  declared  by  the  doctors  of  the  same
society,  a  true  exposition  of  the  Catholic  faith,
in  the  next.  Surely  the  popes  cut  a  poor  figure
with  their  infallibility,  if  we  may  believe  the  hor-
ror which  some  Catholics  now  express  at  certain
doctrines.  They  must  have  greatly  erred  who
allowed  it  lawful  to  murder  persons  who  were  ex-
communicate, who  pretended  to  dispense  king-
doms, depose  princes,  and  absolve  subjects,  from
their  allegiance ;  who  asserted  the  perfection  of
scripture,  and  the  communion  of  infants,  and  who
condemned  communion  in  one  kind,  the  wor-
ship of  images  and  the  title  of  universal  bishop.
What  are  we  to  think  of  the  infallibility  of  a
Church  which  will  declare  the  man  who  shall
read  the  new  Testament  accursed ;  and  pronounce
to  every  one  who  shall  read  the  revelations  of
St.  Bridget  ten  times,  a  plenary  indulgence  of  all
sin ;  if  a  woman  with  child,  safe  deliverance  ;  and
whether  man  or  woman,  escape  from  purgatory
themselves,  and  a  rescue  for  their  father  and  mo-
ther if  they  are  tormented  in  those  flames.  We
have  our  own  reasons  also  for  rejecting  this  pre-
tended infallibility.  We  think  that  every  man
and  every  church  is  fallible,  but  that  the  scriptures


17T

are  an  infallible  rule.  The  christian  religion  is
in  no  way  unreasonable,  but  commends  itself  to
mankind.  There  may  be  many  things  which
natural  reason  could  not  have  discovered,  but
which  when  discovered  are  perfectly  plain.  We
may  not  be  fully  able  to  comprehend  all  that  is
revealed  to  us,  or  to  remove  all  the  difficulties
that  attend  revealed  religion,  (and  who  would
expect  to  do  so  ^)  but  reason  has  done  more  to  re-
move difficulties  and  vindicate  truth,  than  ever
infallibility  did.  In  all  ordinary  and  necessary
points  of  doctrine  and  morals,  a  wayfaring  man
will  not  materially  err,  who  takes  the  scriptures  in
their  plain  sense  as  his  guide,  and  in  many  points
in  which  he  would  err,  those  who  know  better
can  put  him  right.  But  it  is  treating  men
like  brutes  and  irrational  creatures,  to  say  that
God  has  made  a  revelation  to  them,  but  that  they
are  forbidden  to  examine  it.  Man  is  not  infal-
lible, nor  ought  we  to  allow  any  human  being  to
exercise  that  authority  over  us,  which  such  an
infallibility  assumes.  The  judgment  of  wise  and
good  men  is  to  be  respected,  and  we  must  be  slow
to  set  up  our  judgment  against  that  of  the  Church
or  its  teachers,  but  we  are  not  tied  and  bound  to
the  opinions  of  others.  The  right  use  of  reason  and
the  advantages  of  it  in  elevating  our  sentiments
and  enjoyments  have  been  too  happily  illus-
trated in  the  history  of  our  country,  to  allow  such
an  imposition  to  gain  ground  amongst  us.    Many

N


178

of  the  most  valuable  blessings,  which  the  common
instinct  of  humanity  prompts  us  to  hope,  and
which  religion  instructs  us  to  believe,  reason  and
grace,  applying  the  true  principles  of  science  to
the  investigation  of  moral  truth,  have  taught  us
to  knoio  to  be  true.  By  the  right  use  of  reason,
we  have  discovered  that  many  things  in  religion
and  morals,  which  an  impious  infallibility  taught,
and  a  stupid  credulity  believed  to  be  true,  were
mere  impostures ;  and  that  many  moral  truths
and  doctrines  of  the  christian  religion,  which  the
same  infallibility  and  credulity  taught  and  be-
lieved to  be  false,  are  verities  of  the  greatest  con-
sequence, to  the  independence  and  wellbeing  of
mankind  in  this  life,  and  for  their  preparation  for
happiness  in  the  next.

Q.  But  where  was  the  Church  before  the  Re-
formation and  the  Protestant  Religion  ?

A.  We  also  ask  one  question,  where  was  the
true  Philosophy  before  Sir  Isaac  Newton  ?  till
he  discovered  and  settled  the  true  theory  of  the
earth  and  the  heavens,  men  were  under  the  most
palpable  mistakes  and  errors  ;  but  still  they  man-
aged to  plant  and  sow,  and  enjoy  the  comforts
of  life,  though  they  supposed  that  the  earth  stood
still.  Who  will  therefore  say  that  the  discove-
ries of  that  great  man,  who  drew  the  veil  from
the  mysteries  of  nature,  were  not  of  infinite  im-
portance to  science  and  to  humanity  ?  It  argues
no  love  of  truth  to  start  such  questions  as  these,


J  79

and  to  attempt  to  strangle  the  truth  by  means  of
them.  We  mig;ht  as  well  ask,  where  was  salvation
before  the  Roman  Catholic  Church  had  its  uni-
versal monarchy,  since  there  is  now  no  salvation
out  of  it  ?  Where  was  the  Jewish  Church,  during
many  of  its  great  apostacies  ?  It  was  then  among
the  Jews,  though  not  visibly.  When  a  prophet
thought  there  was  only  one  saint  and  himself  that
one,  God  saw  and  knew  thousands.  The  Ro-
man Catholic  Church  made  a  great  and  flagrant
apostacy,  but  the  Church  of  Christ  and  the  light
of  the  gospel  were  never  wholly  extinguished  in
the  greatest  darkness,  that  through  the  infallibi-
lity of  the  universal  monarch,  overwhelmed  it.
The  seven  thousand  in  Israel  were  not  separated
into  another  visible  church,  but  during  the  great
apostacy  maintained  their  .integrity  among  the
nation  of  the  Jews,  who  were  still,  in  a  certain
sense,  the  church  and  people  of  God.  Where
was  the  true  Church  in  the  time  of  Manasseh,
when  the  ten  tribes  were  carried  away,  and  only
the  tribes  of  Judah  and  Benjamin  left,  and  they
«o  wholly  given  to  idolatry,  that  even  the  altar  of
God  in  the  temple  was  destroyed,  and  in  its  place
were  erected  altars  to  Baal,  the  sun,  the  moon,
and  the  host  of  heaven  ?  God  had  then  a  Church
though  not  visibly  distinguished  from  the  rest  of
the  nation,  as  is  apparent  from  the  fact  that  when
Josiah  began  to  reign  at  eight  years  of  age,  there
was  a  party  of  true  Israelites,  who  with  their  young

N  2


180

king  effected  a  thorough  religious  reformation.
There  Avas  true  gold  mixed  Avith  the  alloy  in  the
Roman  Catholic  Church  before  Luther,  and  when
Luther  appeared,  that  true  gold  appeared  in  Ger-
many, There  was  true  gold  in  the  Church  of
England,  before  the  Reformation,  for  there  were
many  thousands  and  tens  of  thousands,  who,  like
Wickliffe  lived  and  died  in  the  Roman  Catho-
lic Church,  abhorring  its  abominations ;  and
when  the  Reformation  was  effected  and  drew^  the
veil  from  the  mysteries  of  grace,  the  visible  and
true  Church  of  Christ  appeared.

Q.  Are  there  any  other  orders  in  the  Roman
Catholic  Church  *?

A.  Yes,  the  monastic  orders  and  religious  so-
cieties, always  considered  as  the  principal  support
of  the  Papacy.  The  Reformation  swept  away
from  protestant  countries  a  shoal  of  Franciscans,
Dominicans,  and  other  orders,  and  the  Church  of
Rome,  therefore,  found  it  necessary  to  erect  a
new  order,  which  should  be  particularly  devoted
to  the  Roman  See,  and  could  act  with  or  with-
out religious  establishments.  This  was  the  -ori-
gin of  the  famous  religious  fraternity  of  the
Jesuits,  composed  from  the  ruins  of  the  other
orders  and  zealots  in  all  countries,  who  were
the  abettors  of  the  unlimited  supremacy  of  the
pope.  Ignatius  Loyala  was  the  fortuitous  head
of  this  conspiracy.  The  whole  order  is  divided
into  three  classes.    The  professed  members  who.


181

where  they  arc  tolerated,  live  in  professed  houses,
and  beside  other  oaths,  take  a  vow  of  unUmited
obedience  to  the  pope  :  the  scholars,  who  are
wealthy,  and  instruct  the  youth  in  colleges  and
schools ;  and  the  novices  who  live  in  houses  of
probation.  Tiie  professed  members  are  the  gen
nine  Jesuits,  men  of  the  world,  acquainted  with
all  the  transactions  of  life,  commerce,  law,  and
politics.  The  companions  and  assistants  of  this
order,  who  are  not  professed  members,  are  very
numerous.  The  secrets  of  the  society  are  not
revealed,  even  to  all  the  professed  members.
Those  who  are  intrusted  with  the  grand  myste-
ries, and  set  apart  to  transact  their  affairs,  em-
ploy, at  their  pleasure,  the  dangerous  doctrines
of  philosophical  probability,  and  mental  reserva-
tion to  carry  on  their  desperate  projects.  They
endeavour  to  secure  the  high  places  of  authority
in  the  Roman  Catholic  Church.  In  former  times,
especially  in  France,  they  were  attacked  and
exposed  by  Catholic  writers,  and  for  the  sake  of
humanity,  we  would  hope  that  their  enemies  must
have  aggravated  their  crimes  and  wickedness ;  but
now,  when  they  are  not  restrained  by  the  tempo-
ral power,  and  where  the  inquisition  is  abolished,
they  are  ascendant  among  Catholics.  They  are
the  connecting  power  between  the  regulars  and
the  secular  clergy,  and  where  the  latter,  from
their  intercourse  with  their  flocks  and  the  world,
are  disposed  to  shew  a  more  social  disposition,

N.S


\S2

patriotic  spirit,  or  religious  and  pious  temper  than
is  convenient  for  political  purposes,  then  the
great  leading  Jesuits  issue  the  threat  of  letting
loose  the  regulars  upon  the  seculars,  to  supplant
them.  It  M^as  this  domineering  spirit  that  ren-.
dered  the  Jesuits  so  odious  to  many  pious  Catho-
lic bishops,  and  parochial  clergy,  who  have
often  been  disposed  to  make  considerable  refor-
mations in  their  Church.  The  Jesuits  are  the
agents,  not  only  of  religious,  but  political  move-
ments, in  favor  of  the  papal  superstition.*    They

sions and  political  struggles  in  protestant  coun-
tries, and  endeavour  to  obtain,  by  the  squeeze  of
parties,  a  political  influence  over  the  civil  and
ecclesiastical  authorities.  Where  they  have  the
opportunity,  they  denounce  the  institutions,  the
clergy,  the  judges,  the  legislature,  the  executive
authorities,  and  even  the  king  and  his  peroga-
tive  in  protestant  countries.  Where  denuncia-
tion is  not  likely  to  succeed,  they  have  recourse
to  artifice ;  where  controversies  and  discussions
are  likely  to  turn  to  their  account,  they  will  em-

stress  is  to  be  laid  on  matters  of  controversy —
things  that  were  formerly  looked  upon  as  import-
ant, are  no  longer  so,  and  all  Christians  are  to
live  in  brotherly  charity.  Another  method  is
to  set  protestants  to  dispute  with  each  other,  to
suggest  occasions  of  quarrel,  one  while  praising
one  set  of  combatants,  and  the  next  moment
their  opponents,  with  a  view  of  exposing  the
frailties  of  both.  When  their  case  is  desperate,
then  they  will  encourage  infidelity  and  indiffer-
ence to  all  religion,  (the  most  horrible  thing
where  they  are  ascendant )  and  if  the  govern-
ment be  free  or  protestant,  then  republicanism
is  the  best  form  of  government  for  the  liberties
of  mankind ;  though  in  other  cases  republicanism
is  worse  than  death.  By  these,  and  various
other  means,  they  pursue  their  grand  object,  the

supremacy  of  the  pope,  and  the  extension  of  his
jurisdiction  over  the  world.  On  these  accounts
their  estabhshments  have  been  suppressed  in
different  countries.  On  various  occasions  they
have  been  banished  from  our  own  country,  from
Russia,  from  Portugal,  and  from  France.  At  the
late  revolution  in  France,  of  which  they  were
the  chief  cause,  their  establishments  were  sup-
pressed, and  many  of  them  have  been  removed
to  England.  In  France,  by  the  mad  pursuit  of
their  tyranny,  they  have  destroyed  the  principle
of  legitamacy,  as  they  did  in  England,  and  have
hurled  Charles  the  tenth,  as  they  did  James  the
second  from  the  throne.  Intelligent  men  in
France  are  determined  to  have  a  constitutional
government;  they  are  endeavouring  to  encourage
the  study  of  greek  literature,  and  the  moral  sci-
ences, which  have  been  miserably  neglected ;
they  are  beginning  to  circulate  the  new  Testa-
ment, the  greatest  instrument  of  moral  order  and
social  happiness,  but  they  will  never  secure  their
liberty  against  foreign  intrigue  and  domestic
faction,  till  they  have  a  national  Church,  with  a
social  and  well-instructed  clergy,  that  may  har-
monize with  all  their  other  institutions.

Q.  What  are  the  holy  orders  which  are  retain-
ed in  the  Church  of  England  *?

A.  There  are  three,  bishops,  presbyters,  and
deacons.  In  the  Scriptures  we  find  frequent
mention  of  these  three  orders.    Before  our  Lord's


185

ascension,  he  ordained  twelve  apostles  and
seventy  disciples,  who  ministered,  after  the  day
of  Pentecost,  in  the  church  of  Jerusalem.  We
do  not  read  of  the  apostolical  ordination  of  pres-
byters in  the  church  of  Jerusalem,  because  they,
who  first  ministered  there,  had  been  ordained  by
Christ  himself.  We  do  read  of  the  ordination  of
deacons  in  consequence  of  the  increase  of  the
church  and  the  duties  of  the  ministry.  In  other
churches  we  read  of  presbyters  and  deacons
ordained  by  the  hands  of  the  apostles.  Apostles
therefore,  and  presbyters,  and  deacons,  were  the
three  degrees  of  power  in  the  apostolic  churches.
When  the  apostles  were  permanently  removed
by  absence  or  death  from  the  churches,  bishops
or  presiding  presbyters,  raised  to  this  specific
office,  performed  those  apostolical  functions
which  were  intended  to  be  permanent  for  the
order  and  edification  of  the  church,  and  as  suc-
cessors of  the  apostles,  ordained  presbyters  and
deacons,  by  the  imposition  of  hands,  as  the  apos-
tles whom  they  succeeded,  and  by  whom  they
were  authorized,  had  done.

Q.  Do  we  not  read  of  other  spiritual  persons
in  the  early  Churches  '?

A.  Yes,  we  read  of  evangelists,  prophets  and
other  spiritual  persons,  and  great  confusion  and
mistakes  have  arisen  from  confounding  these  per-
sons, who  in  the  apostolic  age  had  peculiar  gifts,
with  the  regular  and  permanent  orders  of  the


186

Church.  These  gifts  were  evidently  contined  to
the  period  between  the  day  of  pentecost  and  the
general  establishment  of  Christianity.  Many  of
the  duties,  performed  by  these  persons,  were  offi-
ces and  services  that  might  be  performed  by  per-
sons not  in  regular  orders.  There  were  also  in  after
ages  many  who,  though  they  had  not  these  gifts,
yet  as  catechists  and  teachers,  performed  many
pious  and  useful  services.  But  in  the  ancient
fathers,  as  in  the  Scriptures,  we  read  of  three
regular  orders,  bishops,  presbyters,  and  deacons.
"  When  your  Captains,  (says  TertuUian)  that  is
to  say,  the  deacons,  presbyters,  and  bishops  fly,
who  shall  teach  the  laity  that  they  must  be  con-
stant?" The  primitive  fathers  clearly  speak  of
three  orders,  deacons  as  the  first,  presbyters  the
second,  and  bishops  the  third  and  highest  order.
We  think,  therefore,  that  we  have  now  in  the
Church  of  England,  the  three  orders  which  had
their  beginning  with  the  Apostles  themselves.

Q.  Has  episcopacy  been  long  established  in
England,  and  has  it  been  invariably  preserved  ?

A.  Yes,  there  is  some  probability  that  it  might
have  been  established  by  some  of  the  Apostles,
or  by  apostolic  Evangelists  in  the  lifetime  of  the
Apostles.  Before  the  end  of  the  second  century,
there  were  christians  in  England  under  the  go-
vernment of  their  bishops.  At  the  council  of
Ariminum  A.  D.  359,  the  English  had  their
bishops.     When  the   Saxons  who   were  pagans


187

conquered  the  country,  tlie  English  retired  into
Wales  under  the  government  of  their  bishops.
When  Austin  the  monk,  sent  by  Gregory  the
Great  about  600  years  after  Christ,  came  to  con-
vert the  Saxons,  he  found  the  ancient  Britons
under  the  government  of  their  bishops.  Under
this  form  Christianity  remained  till  the  Norman
conquest,  and  from  the  Norman  conquest  to  the
present  time,  the  kings  of  England  have  sworn  to
preserve  the  Church  of  England  under  that  form.
For  more  than  1600  years  episcopacy  has  existed
in  England,  and,  for  more  than  1200  years,  there
have  been  archbishops  of  Canterbury  appointed
with  the  consent  of  the  kings  of  England.  While
England  shall  remain  an  independent  nation,  she
will,  we  doubt  not,  have  her  own  clergy,  under
the  ancient  episcopal  form  of  goverment,  which
has  been  preserved  by  God  for  the  unity  and  edi-
fication of  the  Church,  and  the  happiness  and
wellbeing  of  the  nation.

Q.  Were  not  presbyters  called  bishops  in  the
scripture,  and  were  not  all  ministers  on  an  equal-
ity?

A.  The  presbyters  are  called  bishops,  and  so
are  the  Apostles  called  presbyters,  and  sometimes
deacons  and  disciples.  There  is  certainly  a  pro-
miscuous use  of  these  terms,  when  they  are  allud-
ed to  in  an  occasional  manner;  but  who  would  con-
clude from  this  that  every  disciple  was  a  deacon,
every  deacon  a  presbyter,  and  every  presbyter  an


188

apostle,  or  that  disciples,  deacons,  presbyters  and
apostles  were  all  the  same  and  on  an  equality  ?
The  term  bishop  is  a  greek  term,  alluded  to  by
some  latin  writers,  and  the  derivation  of  the  word
would  lead  us  to  conclude,  that  it  expresses  a
distinct  office  of  inspection,  visitation  and  juris-
diction. But  the  actual  use  of  the  word  is  more
important,  and,  whether  we  trace  it  downward
from  the  Apostles  to  the  present  day,  or  backward
from  our  own  time  to  the  commencement  of  Chris-
tianity, we  shall  clearly  find  that  it  was  attributed
to  those,  who  not  only  administered  the  word
and  sacraments  (which  is  the  office  of  all  pres-
byters) but  had  a  distinct  power  of  order  and
jurisdiction  over  the  clergy  and  their  flocks.  The
apostles  exercised  a  general  jurisdiction  over  all
Churches,  during  the  first  propagation  of  the
gospel,  but  when  the  Churches  were  settled  or
the  time  of  their  departure  was  at  hand,  then
bishops  were  appointed  in  the  several  Churches,
as  St.  James  at  Jerusalem,  Titus  in  Crete,  Tim-
othy in  Ephesus,  Polycarp  in  Smyrna,  Ignatius
in  Antioch,  Linus  and  Clement  in  Rome,  and  so
in  all  the  Churches  of  Europe,  Asia,  and  Africa,
the  bishops  as  St.  Jerome  says  "were  the  succes-
sors of  the  apostles."

Q.  But  when  St.  Paul  wrote  to  Timothy  and
Titus  expressly  respecting  order  and  discipline,
did  he  not  speak  of  presbyters  and  deacons  and
not  of  bishops  ?


189

A.  When  he  wrote  to  Timothy  and  Titus  he
wrote  to  bishops  who  had  received  their  authori-
ty and  commision  from  himself.  It  was  not  ne-
cessary, therefore,  to  instruct  them  respecting  the
ordination  of  bishops,  and  probably  while  the
Apostles  were  living  they  generally  retained  that
power  in  their  own  hands.  Those  Churches  over
which  Titus  and  Timothy  presided,  were  then
filled  with  these  Officers.  He  did,  however,  in-
instruct  them  to  ordain  presbyters  and  deacons,
whom  the  Apostles  had  ordained  when  they  were
present,  and  who  were  the  officers  that  the  con-
stant increase  of  disciples  rendered  it  necessary
for  them  to  ordain.  Timothy  and  Titus  had  not
the  miraculous  powers  which  the  Apostles  had
for  censure  and  discipline,  and  yet  they  were  evi-
dently ordained  with  espiscopal  function  to  pre-
side over  others  and  ordain  others,  as  the  Apos-
tles had  done.  To  Timothy  St.  Paul  writes  as  to
one  in  a  superior  office  "  Against  a  presbyter  re-
ceive not  an  accusation,  but  before  two  or  three
witnesses."  And  again  to  Titus,  writing  by  in-
spiration, he  says,  "  For  this  cause  left  I  thee  in
Crete,  that  thou  shouldst  put  in  order  the  things
that  remain  (jurisdiction),  and  shouldst  ordain
(ordination)  presbyters  in  every  city  (a  diocese)
as  I  appointed  thee."  Surely  this  is  decisive
against  an  equality,  and  for  a  presidency.  The
titles  of  the  epistles  to  the  seven  Churches  are
very  important,  because  they  were  written  at  the


190

close  of  the  apostolic  age,  when  the  Churches
were  uniformly  settled.  There  were  manifestly
in  those  Churches,  and  the  adjacent  conntry  many
congregations,  ministers, and  a  considerable  laity.
Yet  the  epistles  are  not  directed  to  the  Churches
or  presbyters  and  deacons,  because  they  were  not
intended  to  convey  a  new  revelation  of  doctrine,
but  exhortations  to  preserve  the  unity  and  purity
of  the  Churches.  These  letters,  therefore,  are
addressed  to  the  angels  or  bishops  of  those
Churches.  Surely  if  there  had  been  no  angels  or
presiding  bishops,  or  if  Christ  had  laid  down  the
law  of  equality,  these  letters  must  have  been  es-
teemed as  spurious,  if  not  addressed  to  the  congre-
gation meeting  in  such  a  city,  not  to  the  ministers
or  consistories  of  those  Churches,  but  to  one  pre-
siding, contrary  to  the  supposed  law  of  equality,
with  authority  and  jurisdiction  over  others,  both
laity  and  clergy.

Q.  But  since  it  is  evident  that  there  were
bishops  in  the  apostolic  Churches,  may  we  not
still  suppose  that  episcopacy  was  not  a  divine  or
apostolical  institution  but  human,  adopted  by  the
Churches  themselves,  in  consequence  of  the  di-
visions that  arose,  expedient  at  that  time,  but  not
universally  or  at  all  times  necessary  ?

A.  This,  or  something  like  this,  is  the  opinion
of  Bulinger,  Calvin,  and  some  others.  But  is  it
possible  to  suppose,  that  episcopacy,  which  pres-
byterians   themselves   confess  to    have  been  so


191

ancient  and  so  catholic,  should  have  been  merely
human?  Beza,  indeed,  supposes  three  kinds  of
episcopacy,  divine,  human,  and  satanic,  but  these
suppositions  are  absurd,  as  the  foundations  of  an
argument  in  such  a  matter.  Can  we  suppose
that  this  office  should  have  been  appointed  in
the  apostolic  Churches,  if  the  Churches  had  not
known  it  to  have  been  sanctioned  by  the  scrip-
ture or  the  apostles  themselves  ?  Could  the
disciples  and  friends  of  the  Apostles,  who  were
the  first  bishops,  have  been  ignorant  of  their  in-
tentions respecting  the  order  and  discipline  of  the
Church  *?  or  would  they  have  adopted  an  order
contrary  to  their  known  intentions  ?  If  episco-
pacy rested  on  no  higher  authority  than  that  al-
lowed by  the  presbyterian  writers  (and  a  lower  it
cannot  have)  it  is  an  institution  not  unworthy  of  re-
spect, and  we  may  suppose  not  less  expedient  now
than  it  was  formerly.  Are  there  no  divisions  among
christians  now  *?  Are  there  no  propensities  in
human  nature  to  vanity,  caprice,  and  distraction,
at  the  present  moment  ?  Did  Calvin's  discipline
succeed  in  his  own  case "?  Is  there  no  necessity  for
leading  men  to  be  placed  in  permanent  offices  in
the  Church  of  Scotland  at  the  present  moment  ?
Would  not  eminent  individuals,  (of  whom  there  are
many)  placed  in  prominent  stations  in  the  Church
of  that  country,  render  that  Church  more  secure,
more  eminent,  and  influential  over  all  classes  ?  It
is  unreasonable   to  suppose,    that   such  an  insti-


192

tution  should  have  arisen,  in  the  manner  in  which
it  is  manifest  it  did  arise,  by  human  authority.
There  can  be  no  more  doubt  of  bishops  in  the
apostoHc  Churches,  than  of  consuls  and  senators
in  ancient  Rome.  It  is  also  certain  that  those
bishops  who  filled  the  first  apostolic  sees,  were
almost  without  exception  martyrs,  not  only  to  the
christian  cause,  but,  to  the  episcopal  office.  In  ev-
ery christian  Church  they  were  the  first  to  suffer.
The  office  was  perilous  and  attended  with  no
worldly  honor.  It  exposed  its  possessors  to  cer-
tain persecution  and  death.  It  is  asserted,  that
more  than  thirty  of  the  first  bishops  of  Rome,  with
one  exception,  suffered  martyrdom.  Now,  if  it
was  a  mere  human  institution,  and  not  necessary
or  divine,  can  w^e  suppose  that  the  early  chris-
tians would  have  elected  their  chosen  friends  and
pastors  to  such  an  office,  and  to  certain  destruc-
tion ?  Can  we  suppose  that  they  would  always
have  avowed  an  unbroken  determination  to  exer-
cise their  functions,  or  that  they  would  have  died,
not  only  as  christians,  but  as  bishops,  if  they  had
not  believed,  as  they  professed,  that  their  office
was  divine  and  apostolical  *?  These  suppositions
are  incredible,  and  they  are  the  only  suppositions
by  which  an  apostolical  episcopacy  can  with  any
face  be  evaded.

Q.  What  do  you  conceive  were  the  real  mo-
tives that  induced  Calvin  to  adopt  the  Presbyte-
rian form  of  o;overment  ?


193


A.  There  is  the  greatest  reason  to  suppose  that
he  did  not  adopt  it  from  conviction  or  choice,  but
that  he  elected  and  defended  it  from  what  he
conceived  to  be  an  overruling  necessity.  In  this
he  was  probably  mistaken,  for  if  he  had  adopted
a  moderate  episcopacy  it  is  highly  probable
that  with  his  transcendent  talents,  he  would
have  carried  the  reformation  not  only  in  Swit-
zerland but  in  France,  and  perhaps  in  Italy
and  in  Spain.  He  certainly  had  many  friends
and  adherents  in  all  these  countries.  Had  the
protestants  of  France  adopted  a  moderate  episco-
pacy, that  is  a  form  of  government  adapted  to  a
monarchy,  the  edict  of  Nantes  would  probably
have  never  been  passed  or  revoked,  for  the  kings
and  nobility  of  France  would  have  adopted  the
protestant  faith.  At  this  moment,  if  the  bishops
and  clergy  of  France  were  to  declare  a  Refor-
mation, their  independence  of  the  pope,  the  mar-
riage of  the  clergy,  the  abolition  of  the  supersti-
tious ceremonies  and  the  confessional,  the  liturgy
in  the  veracular  tongue  and  the  sole  authority  of
scripture  in  matters  of  faith  and  practice,  the
French  nation  and  the  best  and  wisest  part  of  it
would  follow  such  a  reformation.  The  French
do  not  reject  Christianity,  but  in  the  form  and
manner  in  which  it  has  been  presented  to  them
by  the  Jesuits  who  have  represented  Jesuitism  and
Christianity  as  synonimous,  and  who  have  thrown
that  thoughtless  and  sensitive  nation  into  appa-

o


194

rent  infidelity  and  atheism.  Had  not  the  dis-
gusting ambition  of  the  Jesuits,  in  the  courts
of  France,  attempted  to  carry  their  tyranny
with  a  reckless  disregard  of  the  rights  of  con-
science and  of  thought,  there  never  would  have
been  a  Voltaire,  certainly  not  such  a  Voltaire  as
there  was.  The  influence  of  Voltaire,  as  a
scoffer,  is  not  now  what  it  has  been,  and  if  reli-
gion were  presented  to  the  people,  in  a  moderate,
decent,  and  rational  form,  they  would  recover
from  their  apparent  atheism,  and  flippant  scof-
fing would  soon  be  obsolete.  The  protestants  of
France,  with  the  fairest  prospects  of  success,  have
not  made  any  progress,  for  consistories  are  neither
fitted  for  enterprise  nor  suited  to  monarchical
institutions.  Presbyterianism  was  not  established
in  France  by  consistories  but  by  Calvin,  and  in
Scotland  by  Knox.  In  Scotland  the  Church  has
been  united  with  the  State,  and  has  proved  a
great  blessing  to  that  country.  But  the  history
of  the  reformed  Church  in  France  shews,  that  a
body  politic  erected  in  the  bosom  of  a  State,  and
unallied  to  the  supreme  authority,  must  be  sus-
pected, precarious,  and  limited  in  its  usefulness.
Calvin  did  not  begin  the  reformation  in  France
or  Switzerland.  In  the  latter  country  he  was  pre-
ceded by  Zuingle  and  CEcolampadius  who  had
used  every  possible  means  and  entreaty  with
the  bishops  of  their  country  to  sanction  their
orders.     When  ( notwithstanding   all  their   in-


195

treaties,  concessions,  and  promises)  they  failed
of  obtaining  the  sanction  of  the  Roman  Hier-
archy, who  were  now  urged  and  threatened  by
the  popes,  they  thought  it  expedient  to  place  at
their  head  a  perpetual  superintendent.  Calvin,
however,  was  not  a  man  to  be  foiled  by  those
whom  he  despised.  That  he  did  not  reject
episcopacy,  that  he  believed  it  under  some  inter-
pretation to  be  apostolical  is  manifest  from  his
institutes,  his  treatise  on  the  true  reformation  of
religion,  his  voluntary  subscription  to  the  confes-
sion of  St.  Austin,  of  which  episcopacy  was  an
article,  and  his  correspondence  with  Archbishop
Cranmer  and  other  learned  men.  His  disciple
John  Knox,  proposed  to  place  presbyterianism
in  Scotland  under  ten  permanent  superintendents
(that  is  bishops  under  another  name)  but  the
spoilers  were  over  the  prey,  and  he  failed.  When
Calvin  determined  that  a  Church  irregular  in
discipline  was  to  be  preferred  to  one  false  in  doc-
trine and  idolatrous  in  practice,  he  made  the  best
of  his  case.  He  saw  that  presbyters  at  the  first,
under  the  episcopacy  of  the  Apostles,  were  equal ;
that  the  Bohemian  brethren  held  episcopacy
without  the  name  ;  that  the  Church  of  Rome  held
bishops  and  presbyters  to  l)e  of  the  same  order,
and  that  the  bishops  of  Rome,  for  avaricious
purposes,  had  sometimes  invested  presbyters  with
the  power  of  orders  and  confirmation,  and  he
therefore  declared  the  Church  of  Geneva  a  sepa-

o2


196

rate  body  and  introduced  that  form  of  ecclesiasti-
cal government  called  presby  terianism.  In  France,
the  Huguenots  had  as  nearly  as  possible  resem-
bled the  Lollards  in  England.  They  had  been  chief-
ly remarkable  for  their  hostility  to  the  corruptions
and  usurpations  of  the  see  of  Rome,  but  the  refor-
mation of  Calvin  produced  a  remarkable  eifect  on
these  Churches,  and  about  the  middle  of  the  1 6th
century  they  entered  into  the  communion  of  the
Church  of  Geneva.  The  principles  of  ecpiality  were
thus  introduced  into  France,  and  as  they  were
never  controlled  by  an  union  with  the  supreme  au-
thority of  the  state,  and  coming  in  contact  with  the
principles  of  Jesuitism  which  led  directly  to  athe-
ism, they  spread  the  seeds  of  that  anarchy  and  in-
fidelity which  have  desolated  France,  though  the
Jesuits  may  be  the  last  to  acknowledge,  or  the
protestants  to  have  wished,  such  consequences.

Q.  What  were  the  opinions  of  Luther,  with
respect  to  orders  *?

A.  Luther  seems  to  have  been  too  much  occu-
pied with  the  pope  to  have  been  sufficiently  pre-
cise in  this  matter.  It  is  evident,  however,  that
he  had  no  wish  to  disturb  the  orders  of  the
Church,  or  to  subvert  episcopal  ordination  and
o'overnment.  The  Lutheran  Church,  as  it  has
existed  since  the  time  of  Luther,  is  held  by  its
Doctors,  to  be  a  lawful  and  complete  hierarchy,
independent  of  the  laws  and  jurisdiction  of  the
Roman  Pontif,   in  consequence  of  the  treaty  of


197

Passau.  The  main  principle  of  the  Lutheran
Church  is  the  same  as  that  of  all  the  reformed
Churches,  that  the  holy  scriptures  are  the  only
source  from  whence  we  are  to  derive  our  reli-
gious sentiments.  They  have  authorized  formu-
laries and  confessions  of  faith .  They  have  adopted
litanies  for  public  worship,  rites  and  ceremonies.
In  every  independent  state  the  supreme  civil  ruler
performs  the  functions  of  supremacy  in  the
Church.  In  some  Churches  the  government  is
episcopal  as  in  Sweden,  where  there  is  one  Arch-
bishop, and  in  Denmark,  by  superintendents,  with
episcopal  powers.  In  other  states  the  government
is  by  consistories,  but  in  all  cases,  they  admit
that  subordination  and  diversity  of  rank  are  not
only  highly  useful,  but  absolutely  necessary  to
preserve  the  unity  and  communion  of  the  Church.

Q.  Do  you  not  consider  that  it  has  been  a
great  advantage  to  the  Church  of  England,  that
it  is  not  founded  on  the  acts  and  opinions  of  any
individual  ?

A.  It  has  certainly  been  a  misfortune  to  the
Lutheran  and  Reformed  Churches  in  general,
that  they  have  been  founded  too  much  upon  the
acts  and  opinions  of  individuals.  Luther's  par-
ticular opinion  respecting  the  Eucharist,  and  the
various  illustrations  which  he  gave  of  it,  have
led  in  many  cases  to  fanaticism,  and  a  bigoted
communion.  The  extreme  opinions  of  Calvin,
respecting  the  decrees  of  God,  and  the  doctrine

o3


198

of  St.  Augustin,  have  led  to  violent  controversies
and  reactions  of  opinion.  Both  Churches  having
missed  the  true  spirit  of  moderation,  have  fallen
too  much  into  the  modern  neology,  so  prevalent
in  Germany  and  France.  The  Church  of  Eng-
land we  think  has  been  happily  preserved  from
these  extremes.  In  throwing  off  the  usurpation
and  superstitions  of  the  Church  of  Rome,  she
had  no  new  system  of  government  to  erect.  She
maintained  the  ancient  government  of  the  Church,
and  effected  her  separation  from  Romish  idola-
try, with  the  greatest  moderation.  She  has  not
been  tied  down  to  the  opinions  or  interpretations
of  any  individual,  however  illustrious.  Dr.  Bur-
net observes  that  it  is  difficult  to  know  who  were
the  authors  of  any  particular  articles.  At  the
precise  period  of  the  Reformation,  the  articles
of  religion  were  drawn  up  to  oppose  the  decrees
of  the  council  of  Trent ;  they  were  submitted  to
the  learned  and  leading  men  of  the  time ;  the
peculiar  phraseology  and  dogmas  of  individuals
were  as  much  as  possible  avoided,  and  the  dis-
cretion, ability,  and  pacific  spirit,  with  which
these  articles  of  peace  and  unity  were  framed,
are  sufficiently  manifest  in  the  fact,  that  all  the
nonconformists,  all  the  methodists  and  calvinists
of  the  present  day,  refer  and  appeal  to  those
articles  of  the  Church  of  England,  which  are
articles  of  faith  and  practice,  as  decisive  in  mat-
ters of  controversy,  and  the  best  authority  next


199

to  scripture  for  their  opinions.  So  far  as  the
Lutheran  or  any  other  Church  holds  the  scrip
ture  to  be  the  sole  rule  of  faith  and  practice  we
hold  communion  with  them ;  so  far  as  the  Roman
Catholic  Church,  or  any  other  Church,  holds  the
fundamental  doctrines,  we  hold  communion  with
them ;  but  we  have  avowed  the  written  word  of
God  as  our  supreme  law,  and  we  cannot  allow
any  Church  to  legislate  any  other  rule  over  us.
As  a  national  Church,  of  an  independent  nation,
we  have  chosen  such  rites  and  ceremonies,  as  suit
our  country  and  habits  ;  such  as  promote  order,
decency,  and  edification  among  us;  and  which  we
anxiously  cherish  as  "  the  leaves  that  preserve
the  fruit,  the  shell  that  preserves  the  kernel
of  religion  from  contempt."  The  Church  of
England  exists  with  her  endowments,  which
secure  a  learned  and  liberal  education  for  the
clergy,  which  renders  them  independent  of  the
highest,  and  able  to  promote  the  comfort  and
edification  of  the  poorest.  The  Church  and  its
possessions  are  the  patrimony  of  the  laboring
classes,  and  of  the  middle  ranks  of  society,  while
at  the  same  time  they  are  a  rock  of  defence  to
the  aristocracy  and  the  throne.  The  christian
ministry  is  one  of  the  honorable  professions  by
which  the  children  of  persons,  even  in  humble
circumstances,  may  and  do  often  open  to  them-
selves a  road  to  fame  and  christian  usefulness ;
it  is  a  profession  in  which  all  ranks  meet  on  an


200

equality.  The  Church  has  enabled  this  country
to  preserve  its  internal  tranquillity,  almost  with-
out a  police,  affectionately  supported,  it  will
continue  to  do  so;  it  has  contributed  essenti-
ally to  preserve  our  national  independence  from
foreign  intrigue,  with  a  less  standing  army
than  any  other  country  in  the  world,  in  pro-
portion to  its  dignity,  population,  and  power,
and  that  in  defiance  of  the  world,  sometimes
in  league  against  us.  It  has  been  blessed
with  bishops  and  learned  men,  whose  memo-
ries are  in  all  the  churches,  whose  writings  are
the  models  of  our  language  and  the  records
of  our  noblest  sentiments,  who  have  defended  the
liberties  of  their  country,  been  eminent  for  learn-
ing, piety,  and  charity,  and  have  carried  the
cultivation  of  the  moral  sciences  and  of  christian
morality  as  a  science,  to  a  higher  degree  of  ex-
cellence than  was  ever  known  in  the  history  of
any  christian  nation.

Q.  What  is  the  king's  supremacy  in  England  ?

A.  It  is  expressed  in  the  following  oath.*
"  That  the  king's  majesty,  under  God,  is  the  only
supreme  governor  of  the  realm,  and  of  all  other
his  hio-hness's  dominions  and  countries,  as  well
in  all  spiritual  and  ecclesiastical  things  or  causes,
as  temporal,  and  that  no  foreign  prince,  person,
prelate,  state,  or  potentate,  hath,   or  ought  to

*  We  assume  the  old  canonical  oath  as  the  best  text  for  explain-
ing this  subject.


201

have  any  jurisdiction,  power,  superiority,  preem-
inence, or  authority,  ecclesiastical  or  spiritual,
within  his  majesty's  said  realms,  dominions,  and
countries."  This  oath  we  will  consider  first  in  its
defensive  and  negative  assertions  ;  and  secondly
in  its  positive  declarations  and  affirmations.

Q.  What  does  this  oath  solemnly  deny  ?

A.  It  denies  "  that  any  foreign  prince,  person,
prelate,  state,  or  potentate,  hath  or  ought  to  have
any  jurisdiction,  power,  superiority,  preemi-
nence, or  authority,  ecclesiastical  or  spiritual,
within  his  majesty's  said  realms,  dominions,  and
countries."  The  power  expressed  in  this  clause,
the  pope  claimed  in  this  country  by  his  legates
and  commissioners,  and  this  power  he  exercised
with  the  most  impudent  and  tyrannical  usurpa-
tions in  defiance  of  the  remonstrances  of  the
king,  parliament,  clergy,  and  people  of  Eng-
land. The  whole  nation,  therefore,  determined
to  cast  it  off  once  and  for  ever.  And  it  is  worthy
of  remark,  that  this  was  virtually  done  before  the
Reformation .  The  usurpation  of  the  pope  was
boldly  attacked  by  Wickliffe,  and  those  who  em-
braced his  opinions.  His  principles  and  the  cir-
culation of  the  New  Testament  in  English,
produced  a  great  change  among  all  classes.  Many
who  did  not  follow  the  whole  of  Wickliffe 's  creed,
and  indeed  those,  who  persecuted  the  Lollards  as
a  sect,  denied  the  pope's  supremacy  and  juris-
diction   in  England.     Before  the  Reformation,


202

(though  indeed  it  was  the  great  pubHc  step  to-
wards it)  the  national  synods,  the  universities,
the  king  and  parUament,  and  the  people  of  Eng-
land and  Ireland,  declared  and  resolved  (as  the
only  way  of  shaking  off  his  disgusting  usurpation)
that  the  bishop  of  Rome  had  no  greater  jurisdic-
tion within  England,  conferred  upon  him,  by
God,  in  scripture,  than  any  other  foreign  bishop.
For  this,  the  pope  excommunicated  the  king
and  whole  Church  of  England,  before  the  refor-
mation made  by  protestants.  It  is  true  the  Ro-
man Catholics  did  not  deny  the  primacy  of  the
pope's  order,  but  his  supremacy  and  power,  and
if  the  bishop  of  Rome  had  been  contented  with
his  title  of  honor,  he  might  perhaps  have  had  it
to  this  day.  It  is  absurd,  therefore,  to  say,  that
the  protestant  clergy  (as  a  new  race  of  invaders)
drove  out  the  real  possessors  of  the  Church,  its
functions,  and  endowments,  and  made  a  new
Church.  It  was  the  same  Church  of  England
after  the  excommunication  of  the  pope,  that  it
was  before  ;  such  a  thing  as  that  could  make  no
possible  alteration  in  it.  When  Elizabeth,  (after
the  desolations  in  queen  Mary's  reign)  passed
the  act  which  separated  for  ever  (we  hope)  the
nation  and  Church  of  England,  from  the  odious
papal  tyranny,  so  completely  had  the  spirit  of
disgust  and  independence  spread  among  the
clergy  and  their  flocks,  that  of  9400  ecclesias-
tical persons,  but  177,  and  those  chiefly  cathedral


203

and  conventual  clergy,  officially  connected  with
the  see  of  Rome,  left  their  livings  to  continue
"in  their  Romish  idolatry."  We  might  with
more  reason  say,  that  a  new  Church  was  made
at  the  revolution,  when  probably  there  were
more  non-jurors  (and  those  the  flower  of  the
English  clergy)  to  the  supremacy  of  king  Wil-
liam, than  there  had  been  recusants  to  the
supremacy  of  queen  Elizabeth.

Q.  Is  this  defensive  oath  now  absolutely  ne-
cessary ?

A.  Yes,  it  is  more  necessary  than  ever  to  know
who  are  the  true  friends  of  the  religion  and  con-
stitution of  their  country.  The  Roman  Catholics
acknowledge,  and  their  clergy  swear  that  they
acknowledge,  that  there  "is  a  foreign  prince,
prelate,  potentate,  who  hath  and  ought  to  have  a
jurisdiction,  power,  superiority,  pre-eminence,
and  authority  ecclesiastical  and  spiritual  within  his
Majesty's  said  realms,  dominions  and  countries,"
— that  is,  that  they  "  will  ever  henceforth  be  faith-
ful and  obedient  to  the  blessed  Apostle  St.  Peter,
and  to  the  Holy  Roman  Church,  and  to  our*  holy
Father  the  Pope  and  his  canonical  successors.

*  In  the  oath  set  forth  in  the  Roman  Pontifical  by  the  order  of
Pope  Clement  VIII.  instead  of  our  Holy  Father  the  Pope  the
expression  is  and  "  to  our  Lord."  In  the  oath  taken  by  the  French
bishops  some  other  clauses  are  omitted,  as  "  Heretics,  schismatics
and  rebels  to  our  said  Lord,  or  his  aforesaid  successors,  I  will
to  ray  power  persecute  and  oppose."


204

That  any  counsel  which  they  may  intrust  to  them
by  themselves,  their  messengers  or  letters  they
will  not  knowingly  reveal  to  any  to  their  preju-
dice ;  that  they  will  aid  them,  saving  their  order,
to  preserve  and  defend  the  Roman  See  and  the
sovereign  rights  of  St.  Peter^  that  they  will
treat  with  honor,  the  legate  of  the  Apostolic  See
on  his  journeys,  and  assist  him  in  his  necessities  ;
That  they  will  endeavour  to  presej've,  defend^
increase,  and  advance  the  rights,  honors,  pri-
vileges, and  authorities  of  the  holy  Roman
Church,  of  our  holy  Father  the  Pope  and  his
successors."  Besides  this,  the  various  orders  and
congregations  take  separate  vows  and  oaths,  and
the  Jesuits  take  again  an  especial  oath  of  unre-
served obedience  to  the  pope,  to  go  wherever  he
may  direct  them,  to  promote  and  advance  the
universal  dominion  of  the  universal  monarch.  It
is  therefore  the  grossest  hypocrisy  on  the  part  of
the  members  of  that  Church,  in  which  the  swear-
ing of  obedience  to  the  pope  is  an  article  of  their
creed,  and  which  has  commanded  more  vows  and
secret  oaths,  than  all  other  Churches  in  the  world ,-
to  pretend  such  sympathy  with  tender  consciences
and  a  dislike  to  oaths  of  a  particular  kind.  The
sacramentum  is  the  strongest  bulwark  of  human
institutions  ;  it  is  the  end  of  strife  and  the  begin-
ning of  safety,  None  are  better  convinced  of
this  than  many  persons  who,  while  they  propose
the  repeal  of  all  oaths  which  are  to  defend  the


205

religion,  and  with  it  the  monarchy  and  institu-
tions of  their  country  from  usurpation,  are  them-
selves, perhaps,  under  secret  and  solemn  vows
and  oaths  to  promote  that  usurpation  to  the  ut-
most of  their  power.  Since  the  repeal  of  the
test  and  corporation  acts,  (the  worst  possible  kind
of  test,  and  which  nothing  but  absolute  necessity
could  justify)  it  is  more  than  ever  necessary  to
preserve  those  bulwarks  which  impose  no  penal-
ty, and  which  only  require  allegiance  and  fidelity
distinct,  open  and  avowed  to  a  protestant  king
and  coastitution.  The  time  is  come  not  only  to
defend  these  with  oaths  but  with  great  vigilance,
against  the  spiritual  militia,  the  Janizaries  and
factors  of  the  pope.  Had  not  the  constitution  of
the  country  been  defended  with  oaths,  and  among
others  with  the  oath  of  supremacy,  it  had  been
betrayed  long  ago  into  the  hands  of  its  enemies.
Oaths  have  an  influence  on  those  who  are  not
restrained  by  other  obligations.  Oaths  may  be
painful  to  skilful  interpreters  of  oaths,  but  they
restrain  even  such  casuists,  if  not  for  conscience,
yet  for  credit's  sake.*

*  Philosophical  probability  will  carry  those  who  profess  it  to
great  lengths.  We  know  that  the  order  of  Bishops  has  been  the
great  means  of  preserving  the  Protestant  Religion  in  England.
The  order  of  Bishops  in  the  English  Church  and  the  supremacy  of
the  King  are  the  two  great  bulwarks  which  have  defied  the  Pa-
pists, and  how  they  regard  them,  particularly  the  former,  history
sufficiently  attests ;  witness  the  murderous  hate  with  which  they

Q.  What  are  the  positive  affirmations  of  this
oath?

A.  *'  That  the  king's  majesty,  under  God,  is
the  only  supreme  governor  of  this  realm,  and  of
all  other  his  highness'  dominions  and  coun-
tries, as  well  in  all  spiritual  or  ecclesiastical
things  or  causes  as  temporal."  This  is  an  oath
which  not  only  every  churchman,  but  every  wise

seized  and  slaughtered  Hooper,  Ridley,  Latimer,  and  Cranmer,  on
their  recovery  of  power ;  witness  the  Cain-like  malignity  with
which  the  Jesuits,  when  they  thought  they  had  secured  power
under  King  James,  seized  the  seven  patriotic  Bishops ;  witness
the  pleasure  with  which  the  suppression  of  the  twelve  Irish  Bish-
opricks  was  received  an  "  odour  of  a  sweet  smell,  a  sacrifice  accept-
able, well-pleasing;"  witness  the  confession  of  one  of  their  advocates
at  the  time  of  the  reformation  "  I  could  be  contented  there  were  no
Priests  ( i.  e.  Popish  Priests)  in  England,  so  there  were  no  Bishops
there  ".  Yet  it  is  now  doing  no  injury  to  the  Church  of  England
to  endeavour  to  deprive  the  Bishops  of  their  titles  which  they  enjoy
according  to  law,  while  at  the  same  time  the  same  titles  are  revived
to  those  who  hold  them  contrary  to  law.  It  would  be  no  injury
to  vote  unanimously  the  suppression  of  all  the  cathedral  estabHsh-
ments  in  England  from  the  Church  of  England,  and  to  buy  them
up,  or  vote  them  for  the  Church  of  Rome.  All  this  is  no  violation
of  an  oath,  under  an  interpretation  or  dispensation.  We  know
however  the  value  of  episcopal  ordination,  and  our  country  we
sincerely  believe  will  draw  to  it  more  and  more.  What  the  Ro-
man Catholics  think  of  the  king's  supremacy  is  evident  from
Bossuet's  assertion  that  it  is  the  only  thing  among  the  Protestants
in  England  that  has  not  changed,  and  no  sophistry  will  make  it
mutable,  notwithstanding  the  authorised  system  of  another  su-
preme head,  "  to  pluck  up,  destroy,  dissipate,  ruinate,  plant,  and
build."


207

and  conscientious  protestant  ought  to  take.
There  are  many  that  object  to  this  oath,  who
probably  never  read  it,  much  less  considered
why  and  for  what  important  objects  it  was
required.  There  are  those  who  object  to  the
prerogative,  which  the  laws  have  vested  in  the
crown,  and  who  maintain  that  the  separation  of
Church  and  state  ought  to  be  personal  and  per-
petual. We  think  there  are  many  persons  who
do  not  understand  the  alliance  which  the  oath
affirms,  and  consequently  misrepresent  the  case
altogether.  In  the  abstract  we  admit  as  willingly
as  those  persons  do  a  distinction  between  the
Church  and  the  state.  They  are  not  necessarily
the  same,  but  that  is  not  a  reason  why  they  should
always  be  separate,  and  in  the  present  state  of
Christendom,  and  the  relations  of  this  country,
there  are  the  highest  reasons  why  they  should
be  legally  and  firmly  miited.  The  direction  of
every  community  is  by  power,  and  if  that  power
be  left  undefined  by  law,  it  will  still  exist,  and
sooner  or  later  become  absolute.  The  exercise
of  the  christian  religion  is  a  power,  and  the  direc-
tion of  the  exercise  of  the  christian  religion  is
a  power  and  authority,  and  if  left  undefined,
it  will  still  rest  somewhere^  and  though  for  a
short  time  the  secret  may  not  be  discovered,  the
tyranny  of  an  undefined  power,  will  soon  declare
itself.  Those  who  direct  the  state,  must  have  a
perception  of  the  powers  within  the  state.    They


208


will  soon  perceive  the  powers  ecclesiastical,  and
the  powers  ecclesiastical,  whatever  they  may  pre-
tend, will  soon  see  the  value  of  the  civil  power
and  the  royal  power,  and  to  obtain  their  support
will  enter  into  league  and  covenant  with  them.
It  is  therefore  of  the  greatest  consequence  that
that  alliance  or  league  should  not  be  secret  but
known  ;  not  only  personal,  but  legal ;  not  vague
and  undefined,  but  limited  and  invariable ;  and
that  on  oath ;  not  between  fellow-citizens  and
foreign  potentates,  but  between  the  king  and  his
people.  The  investment  of  the  supreme  power
ecclesiastical,  as  well  as  civil,  in  the  crown,  so
long  as  the  crown  shall  remain  protestant,  has,
"  under  God,"  preserved  the  gospel  light,  the
civil  and  religious  principles  of  this  country,
the  exclusion  of  foreign  intrigue  and  usurpation  ;
and  the  true  spirit  of  independence  has  turned
upon  it.  It  is  in  vain  to  argue  with  those  persons
who  object  to  oaths,  because  they  do  not  wish  to
be  tied  to  any  principles,  or  with  those  who  wish  to
choose  what  oaths  shall  be  administered  to  them  by
the  state.  But  there  are  virtuous  and  conscientious
individuals,  who  scruple  to  sanction  this  oath,
though  they  admit  that  the  protection  which  it
designs  to  secure  is  necessary.  To  such  persons
we  sincerely  say  we  mean  as  they  do,  by  a  Church,
a  society  united  in  a  public  form  of  government,
and  distinguished  from  all  other  societies  by  the
exercise  of  the  christian  religion,  the  profession


209

of  the  gospel,  the  administration  of  the  word  and
sacraments  and  union  and  communion  under  law-
ful pastors;  we  mean  as  explicitly  as  they  do,  that
no  king  can  ordain  those  pastors,  administer
those  sacraments,  alter  that  word,  or  destroy  that
union.  If  the  Church  existed  under  a  Pagan
government,  we  will  allow  that  no  union  could
take  place,  and  moreover,  that  to  avoid  an  alli-
ance with  idolatry,  the  Church  and  its  professors
must  be  prepared  to  suffer  persecution  unto  death.
The  Roman  Emperors  persecuted  the  christians
not  because  they  maintained  their  own  religion,
but  because  they  would  not  admit  the  principle  of
Roman  policy,  the  freedom  and  intercommunity
of  all  religions  which  the  philosophers  of  that  day
(professing  themselves  wise  and  becoming  fools)
considered  necessary  to  the  public  utility.  The
government  under  which  christians  then  lived,
was  not  christian,  and  consequently  they  could
not  join  with  the  state,  and  persecution  and  not
toleration,  under  the  principles  of  comprehension
and  intercommunity,  was  the  consequence.  But
when  the  state  became  christian,  the  case  changed
naturally,  and  of  itself.  A  christian  nation  is
distinguished  by  the  exercise  and  profession  of
the  christian  religion.  Why,  then,  should  not
the  princes,  nobles,  clergy,  and  people,  where  all
are  christians,  exercise  the  christian  religion  as  a
nation  without  violating  scripture,  or  conscience,
or  the  laws  of  Jesus  Christ  ?     If  the  members  of

p


210

the  state  be  christians,  and  if  the  christian  reli-
gion be  divine  and  benevolent,  and  if  the  Church
be  in  no  way  by  foreign  jurisdiction  and  allegi-
ance alienated  from  the  state,  one  would  suppose
that  in  proportion  as  the  union  became  legal  and
cordial,  the  influence  of  the  christian  religion
and  the  happiness  of  the  people  would  be  in-
creased. So  far  from  the  excellence  and  purity
and  moral  efficacy  of  the  christian  religion  being
an  argument  against  the  alliance  it  is  the  strong-
est argument  for  it,  if  not  in  the  eyes  of  particular
individuals  with  particular  feelings,  certainly  in
the  judgment  of  wise  and  christian  legislators.  If
conscience  be  as  clearly  a  part  of  human  nature
as  the  appetites,  and  if  the  state  (as  all  expe-
rience shews)  must  have  the  support  of  religious
sanctions  on  the  conscience,  the  more  harmoni-
ously religion  is  combined  with  the  state,  without
imparing  its  truth,  the  happier  that  state  must
be.  The  State  and  the  Church,  considered  in
the  abstract,  are  not  the  same  thing.  There  are
differences  in  their  nature  and  administration,  (as
there  are  between  mind  and  matter,  though
these  differences  do  not  prevent  their  union)  and
the  real  difference  between  civil  and  spiritual
functions  must  be  preserved.  Orders  therefore
are  preserved  in  the  Church,  and  when  the
Church  is  allied  to  the  state,  those  orders  for  the
preservation  of  this  distinction,  must  be  represen-
ted in  the  state.     The   supremacy  of  kings  in


^211

their  dominions  is  reasonable  in  itself.  It  is  ne-
cessary that  no  foreign  power,  prelate  or  poten-
tate, may  have  or  presume  to  have  any  authority
over  them  or  their  subjects.  It  is  necessary  also
that  no  domestic  faction  may  form  "  an  imperium
in  imperio"  and  disturb  the  peace  of  society.
Upon  every  principle  of  reason,  civil  liberty,  and
christian  charity,  all  persecution  for  religious
opinions  is  hateful  and  unjustifiable ;  but  the
christian  religion  ought  not  to  be  exercised  in  such
a  manner  as  to  disturb  civil  society  or  endanger
the  christian  religion  itself, which  is  a  part  and  par-
cel of  the  law  of  the  land,  and  necessary  to  secure
the  enjoyments  and  happiness  of  a  christian  peo-
ple. However  men  may  talk  against  the  protec-
tion of  the  christian  religion,  by  the  law  of  the
land  or  the  supremacy  of  the  king  in  things  spi-
ritual as  well  as  temporal,  they  find  practically
that  they  cannot  live  as  christians  in  the  enjoy-
ment of  their  rights  and  conscience  without  an
appeal  to  these  shocking  principles.  The  most
pure  congregation  that  ever  derived  their  religion
(as  they  say)  not  from  men  or  angels,  but  from  the
throne  of  God,  must  if  they  have  any  rules,  any
common  consent,  any  deed  of  trust,  and  especially
any  endowments^  acknowledge  the  supremacy  of
the  king.  When  any  disputes  arise  respecting
these  endowments  and  the  due  admimsirati07i
of  the  christian  religion  in  chapels  as  well
as  churches,  the  most  religious  dissenters  refer

matters  of  pure  relig-ioii,  their  solemn  deeds,

the  chHstian  religion  according  to  law.  The

ing to  the  laws  of  the  congregation,  whatever
they  may  be,  provided  they  be  not  blasphemous
or  do  not  contravene  the  law  of  the  land,  and  just
so  the  king  exercises  his  authority  over  the  exter-

of  that  Church,  which  cannot  contravene  the  laws
of  the  land,  because  they  are  happily  a  part  and
parcel  of  them,  and  perfectly  known  and  under-
stood.*    There  never  was  a  religion  connected

would  not  renounce  their  faith,  to  be  burnt,  and

an  iniquity  into  execution,  the  prelate,  and  the

sed to  this  sort  of  supremacy,  and  partly  to  keep
out  persecution  and  foreign  tyranny,  and  partly
