religion,  and  partly  to  secure  to  all  societies  of
christians  a  toleration  with  the  legal  enjoy-
ment of  their  laws  and  endowments,  the  nation
has  legalized  the  ancient  national  church  with
its  laws,  institutions  and  endowments.  No  king,
no  faction,  no  foreign  power  hath  or  ought  to
have  any  right  to  interfere  with  the  dissenter  in
his  legal  privileges,  and  no  king,  faction,  or  fo-
reign power,  ought  to  interfere  with  the  Church
with  her  legal  privileges,  for  the  king  and  the  law
are  supreme  to  protect  us  both.

Q.  But  if  the  establishment  of  religion  by  law
is  reasonable,  is  it  not  unscriptural  ?

A.  In  matters  of  this  sort  we  think  that  reason
and  scripture  are  not  often  opposed.  They
are  not  opposed  in  this  case  if  the  religion
established  by  law  recognizes  the  scripture.  If
the  religion  established  by  law  rejects  the
scripture,  then  it  is  not  the  Christian  religion
which  is  established,  and  the  Christian  dissenter
cannot  urge  his  conscientious  objections.  But
scripture  is  scripture,  and  if  the  scripture  forbid
the   establishment   of  the   christian    religion  by


215

law,  it  must  say  so  in  plain  and  express  terms.
If  any  persons  in  this  country  think  this  point
clearly  proved  (as  we  certainly  do  not)  out  of
the  scripture,  then  they  have  the  full  liberty  to
exercise  their  own  religion  in  their  own  way,
make  their  own  endowments,  establish  their  own
schools  and  colleges,  build  their  own  churches,
sit  under  their  own  vine  and  their  own  fig  tree,
none  making  them  afraid ;  but  all  these  enjoy-
ments are  still  under  the  supremacy  of  the  king
and  according  to  law.  It  seems  hard,  however,
that  while  they  have  the  exclusive  enjoyment  of
their  privileges,  the  church,  because  she  simply
acknowledges  a  legal  fact,  the  supremacy  of  the
king,  should  not  have  the  exclusive  enjoyment
of  her  vineyard.  The  church  does  not  interfere
with  dissenters  in  the  enjoyment  of  their  rights
and  endowments,  does  not  covet  a  share  in  their
colleges,  schools  of  learning  or  chapel  patronage,
provided  they  do  not  interfere  with  the  rights
and  property  of  the  Church,  and  then  the  Church
appeals  against  the  dissenter  as  the  dissenter
does  against  his  brother  dissenter  to  the  law  and
to  the  king.  Why  are  the  dissenters  (those  who
use  such  language,)  if  they  are  really  convinced
that  their  principles  are  more  pure,  less  mundane,
more  elevated  than  ours — that  purity,  holiness,
and  learning,  are  on  their  side,  and  vanity,
ambition,  and  ignorance  on  ours,  not  contented
with   the   calm   and   dignified    consciousness   of


216

their  own  superiority  ?   But  further,  witli  regard
to  the  authority  of  scripture,  we  think  as  revela-
tion does  not  assert  that  christian  kings  should
not  have  a  supremacy  in  ecclesiastical  causes,  as
well  as  civil,    and  as  "  the  blessed  Apostle,    St.
Peter  "  does  assert  generally  without  exceptions,
the  authority  of  kings  over  their  people,  that
this  supremacy  may  be  fairly  inferred.    If  scrip-
ture is  silent,  then  reason  and   philosophy  must
be  our  guides.     The  will  of  God  may  be  learnt
in  many  things,  and  those  very  important  things,
even  where  the  scripture  is  silent.     The  scrip-
ture supposes  that  reason  and  natural    religion
will  teach  us   many   important   lessons  without
the  aid  of  revelation,  or  at  least  in  subserviency
to  it.  If  God  has  established  Christianity,  and  de-
clares that  it  is  for  all  nations^  is  it  unreasonable
or  unscriptural  for  all  nations,  and  every  nation^
(as  well  as  all  individuals)  to  accept  and  recog-
nize it  ?   If  christian  states,  consulting  the  unity
and  wellbeing  of  their  members,  knowing  that
morality  cannot  be  secured  by  laws,  or  enforced
by  police,  choose  the   christian  religion  as  the
most  intelligible  and  the  best,  and  that  religion
secures  the  bonds   and  alliances,  which  preserve
and  bless  society,  then  we  contend  that  God  by
these  means  declares,  in  the  course  of  his  provi-
dence, that  the  maintenance  of  the  christian  reli-
gion is  according  to  his  will,  and  that  a  moral
obligation    ensues  to    kings   and  legislators,  to


2\7


defend  and  preserve  it.  All  christians  profess
to  believe  that  the  Church  is  providentially
not  miraculously  preserved  in  the  world,  and
can  we  suppose  that  the  Church  can  be  better
preserved  in  its  external  state,  than  by  those
christian  princes  and  christian  laws,  by  which
God  preserves  society  itself?  When  the  pres-
byterians  were  in  power,  they  called  upon
the  parliament  to  perform  its  religious  duties.
Scripture  then  spoke  as  expressly  for  religion
established  by  law,  as  it  does  now  against
it,  and  the  parliament  religiously  legalized  the
directory.  When  the  independents  acquired
a  share  in  the  political  power  of  the  state,
they  also  quoted  scripture  to  the  same  effect,
acknowledged  the  protector  as  supreme,  refer-
red to  king  David  and  king  Solomon,  and
called  upon  their  godly  governors  to  defend
and  support  their  godly  discipline,  with  that
power  with  which  the  Almighty  had  invested
them.*    Of  the  bulls,  legates,  and  commissioners

of  the  pope,  and  of  their  religious  use  of  the  civil
power,  it  is  unnecessary  to  speak,  though  we  are
now  told  that  his  kingdom  is  not  of  this  world.
It  is  not,  however,  supremacy  of  this  sort  of

macy of  caprice,  but  of  law,  defined  within  cer-

therefore,  men  may  pretend  that  Churches  must
be  governed  by  spiritual  means,  that  religious
decisions  must  be  made  as  those  at  the  council
of  Jerusalem  were  made  ( that  is  by  persons  di-
vinely inspired)  that  a  christian  man's  liberty
is  lost,  if  laws  are  passed  for  the  government  of
the  Church ;  to  all  these  and  the  like  assertions
(which  imply  no  less  than  an  apostolical  and
plenary  power)  we  reply  that  the  advocates  of
them  do  not  themselves  trust  to  them ;  that
human  laws,  in  all  external  regulations,  whether
ecclesiastical  or  civil,  will  be  necessary,  so  long
as  men  are  liable  to  passions  and  interest,  unless

219

congregation,  and  every  individual,  on  every
occasion,  determine  who  is  right  and  who  is
WTong,  and  add  to  that  revelation  a  power  to
enforce  it.  Moreover,  we  affirm  that  since  God
has  made  known  to  us  that  all  men  must  live  in
families  and  nations,  and  in  their  social  relations
and  christian  communion,  must  live  decent-
ly according  to  laMS,  we  are  in  conscience
bound  to  obey  human  laws,  which  do  not  oppose
the  law  of  God,  revealed  to  us  in  scripture.  If
we  live  under  a  tyranny,  we  must  take  the  conse-
quence and  suffer  persecution,  till  that  tyranny
be  removed,  but  if  we  enjoy  a  rational  liberty,  we
must  endeavour  to  preserve  it.  By  maintain-
ing in  England  the  principle  of  law  in  Church
and  state  ;  by  sanctioning  the  constitution  of  the
christian  religion,  in  its  discipline  and  doctrines  ;
doctrines  which  reveal  the  great  principles  of
moral  truth,  and  discipline  consecrated  to  its
administration,  the  best  interests  of  all  classes
have  been  consulted,  the  doctrines  of  Christianity
have  been  rescued  from  superstition,  and  disci-
pline freed  from  enthusiasm  and  tyranny,  has
been  executed  with  moderation  and  charity.
For  these  reasons,  as  well  as  in  opposition  to
foreign  usurpation  and  domestic  faction,  our
motto  is  religion  and  law.

Q.  If  your  opinions  are  so  decided  in  favor  of
one  system,  do  you  not  condemn  all  who  differ
from  it  ?


220

A.  In  a  certain  sense  all  opposing  sentiments,
especially  on  subjects  of  importance,  must  con-
demn each  other,  but  this  is  no  reason  why  the
investigation  of  truth  should  not  lead  to  conclu-
sions, or  why  an  honest  man  should  not  be  guided
by  his  convictions.  As  for  judging  the  motives
or  eternal  salvation  of  other  men,  that  is  not  the
province  and  ought  not  to  be  the  inclination  of
any  candid  inquirer.  Differences  of  opinion
respecting  systems  of  faith  do  sometimes  inter-
fere with  the  cordiality  of  private  intercourse,
but  this  is  a  frailty  and  defect  which  never  tends
to  promote  the  cause  of  truth.  The  assertion
cannot  be  too  often  reiterated,  that  social  and
christian  virtues  are  not  confined  to  sect,  or  par-
ty. We  allow  to  the  Roman  Catholic  all  the
advantage  he  can  desire  from  the  bigoted  quib-
ble that  there  is  salvation  out  of  our  Church,
and  not  out  of  his.  We  allow  to  the  dissenters,
that  while  we  are  persuaded  that  our  doctrine
and  discipline  are  scriptural,  apostolical,  highly
expedient,  and  need  not  seriously  offend  the  con-
sciences of  any,  we  have  also  another  persuasion,
that  the  kingdom  of  heaven  is  open  to  all  be-
lievers ;  that  no  church  can  alter  the  terms  of
repentance  and  faith,  and  that  every  man  will  be
judged  by  his  conscientious  and  predominant  love
of  truth.  Many  things  that  are  laudable,  are  not
absolutely  necessary,  and  great  defects  and  even
unsightly  deformities  in  religion,  may  not  destroy


221

the  Christianity  of  a  church,  or  an  individual.
Still  human  nature  is  what  it  always  has  been,
and  these  charitable  convictions  are  not  to  shut
our  eyes  to  the  merits  and  demerits  of  various
schemes,  which  design  or  weakness  may  from
time  to  time  suggest.  One  of  these  schemes
is  the  comprehension  and  intercommunity  of
all  religious  persuasions  in  one  fixed  society.
Experience  has  shewn,  that  this  imagination  is
impracticable,  and  that  many  who  praise  it,  have
no  intention  to  apply  it  to  themselves.  Intercom-
munity, when  not  only  opinions,  but  sentiments
and  the  strongest  feelings  and  habits  are  opposed,
can  only  end  (such  is  human  nature)  in  mutual
hostility  and  triumphant  persecution.  Respect
for  others,  and  respect  for  ourselves,  without  pre-
tending on  our  part  or  expecting  of  others,  a
compromise  of  genuine  sentiments,  is  the  practi-
cal point  at  which  all  conscientious  men  must
aim,  and  at  which  they  must  rest.  Within  this
limit,  differences  may  be  reconciled,  and  causes
of  division  removed ;  but  if  mistaken  candour
attempt  to  step  beyond  this  boundary,  mutual
respect,  justice,  and  charity,  by  which  alone  the
true  spirit  and  wholesome  practice  of  entire  tole-
ration can  be  preserved,  will  infallibly  be  en-
dangered.


CHAPTER  IV.


"  By  thee
Founded  in  reason,  loyal,  just,  and  pure,
Relations  dear,  and  all  the  charities
Of  father,  son,  and  brother,  first  were  known.
Far  be  it,  that  I  should  write  thee  sin  or  blame,
Or  think  thee  unbefitting  hoUest  place."


Q.  What  is  the  doctrine  of  the  Church  of
Rome  respecting  marriage  ?

A.  The  Tridentine  fathers  have  determined
the  points  relating  to  marriage  with  great  pre-
cision. With  respect  to  the  contract  itself  they
declare,  that  it  is  the  mamage  union,  within
lawful  degrees,  of  man  and  woman,  which  binds
them  to  live  in  constant  unity  and  love.  To  this
contract  are  required,  the  inward  consent  of  both
parties,  outward  agreement  expressed,  the  obli-
gation and  bond  which  is  effected  by  that  agree-
ment, and  the  consummation  of  wedlock.  They
enact,  that  marriage  is  of  two  kinds,  natural  and
sacramental ;  that  natural  marriage  is  not  an  iu-
vention  of  men,  but  coeval  with  our  nature,  and


223

instituted  of  God  immediately  after  the  creation
of  Eve,  when  he  said,  "  Be  fruitful  and  multiply,
and  replenish  the  earth,"  and  again,  "  Therefore
shall  a  man  leave  his  father  and  his  mother,  and
shall  cleave  unto  his  wife,  and  they  shall  be  one
flesh."  They  further  teach,  that  this  divine  insti-
tution was  recognized  by  Christ,  and  declared  by
him  to  be  indissoluble,  though  the  indissoluble
nature  of  marriage  is  to  be  chiefly  referred  to  its
sacramental  character,  in  which  view  marriage
is  of  greater  excellence,  and  to  be  referred  to
nobler  ends.  For  as  natural  marriage  favors  the
propagation  of  the  human  race,  and  educates
children  as  servants  of  their  Creator,  so  sacra-
mental marriage  raises  up  true  worshippers  of
our  Lord  and  Saviour  Jesus  Christ,  and  extends
the  true  religion.  The  religious  sentiment  of
this  statement  we  respect,  whilst  we  object  to  its
main  position.

Q.  What  are  declared  to  be  the  matter,  form,
institution  and  effects  of  sacramental  marriage  ?

A.  The  matter  assigned  by  the  Roman  doctors,
is  the  inward  assent  of  both  parties,  and  the  form
consists  in  the  words  or  signs  by  which  it  is  ex-
pressed. The  institution,  as  a  sacrament,  is  as-
serted to  be  in  the  words  of  St.  Paul  (Ep.  v.  23.)
where  according  to  the  Latin  version,  he  says,
"  This  is  a  great  sacrament."  Marriage,  there-
fore, though  as  a  mutual  contract  it  rests  upon
the  law  of  nature,  yet,  as  it  is  a  great  sacrament.


224

contains  an  inherent  divine  virtue,  which  sancti-
fies the  lawful  union  of  man  and  woman  ;  gives
grace  to  the  married  pair,  to  perform  the  duties
and  support  the  trials  of  this  estate  in  a  christian
manner,  and  renders  the  union  indissoluble.  All
who  are  not  married  by  their  own  pastor,  are
declared  to  live  in  disobedience  to  the  Church,
and  in  a  continual  state  of  sin.

Q.  Does  the  scripture  quoted,  or  do  these  fic-
tions of  the  Romish  doctors  prove  marriage  to
be  a  sacrament  ?

A.  The  translation  of  the  vulgate  is  "  This  is
a  great  sacrament,"  but  if  we  turn  to  the  original
we  find  the  text  to  be  as  we  translate  it,  "  This
is  a  great  mystery."  A  sacrament  is  a  mystery,
but  in  this  case,  as  in  numberless  others,  terms
are  not  convertible.  The  interpretation,  there-
fore, is  not  legitimate,  and  moreover,  the  con-
text shews  it  to  be  unfounded.  The  apostle  is
merely  employing  a  mystical  argument  or  illus-
tration, to  confirm  the  duties  of  husbands  and
wives,  and  to  explain  the  union  and  communion
of  Christ  and  the  Church.  When  speaking  of
the  christian  Church,  the  apostle  shews  that  it  is
one  body,  the  mosaic  law  being  abolished,  and
Jews  and  Christians  being  one  in  Christ.  This
Church  is  Christ's  spouse,  and  just  as  Christ
loves  the  Church,  and  the  Church  is  obedient
to  Christ,  so  the  husband  is  to  love  the  wife,  and
the  wife  to  reverence  the  husband.     Just  as  a


225

man,  who  should  have  more  than  one  wife,  would
not  live  in  lawful  marriage,  so  there  would  be  an
unchaste  impropriety  in  calling  the  Church  the
spouse  of  Christ,  if  all  the  members  of  it,  Jews
and  Gentiles,  did  not  constitute  one  body.  Here
is  a  double  argument  for  unity  and  love,  both  in
the  Church,  and  between  husband  and  wives,  but
both  the  text  and  context  shew  that  there  can  be
no  institution  of  a  sacrament.  Besides  it  is  a
curious  thing  to  make  thoughts  the  matter,  and
words  the  form  of  a  sacrament ;  for  according  to
such  a  definition,  every  contract  or  promise  might
be  called  a  sacrament.  Such  a  definition  also  is
absurd,  and  calculated  to  encourage  scruple  and
fraud.  Parties  often  marry  against  their  inward
intention  and  desire,  and  according  to  this  sacra-
mental doctrine,  if  the  inward  consent  of  either
party  fail,  the  marriage  may  be  null  and  void,
and  all  persons  who  are  in  that  situation,  may
ever  live  in  uncertainty  about  the  lawfulness  of
their  marriage.  Lewd  and  vicious  men  too,  may
entangle  others,  and  yet,  by  secret  reservation
of  giving  their  consent,  may  keep  themselves  free,
and  where  the  fallacious  doctrines  of  intention
and  reservation  prevail,  as  it  is  to  be  feared  they
often  do,  a  door  is  open  to  every  species  of  perfidy
and  treachery.

Q.  Does  not  Jesus  Christ  declare  marriage  to  be
indissoluble,  and  is  it  not  therefore,  a  sacrament  ?

A.  Jesus  Christ  instructs  us  to  consider  mar-
Q


226

riage  both  as  a  natural  and  divine  institution ;  that
the  human  race  began  by  a  pair,  and  that  they
were  made  by  God's  ordinance  man  and  wife ;
that  divorce  was  sinful,  and  contrary  to  the  will
of  God,  who  had  ordained  this  union  of  one  man
and  woman  in  constant  wedlock.  "What  God  hath
joined  together  let  no  man  put  asunder."  At
the  same  time,  however,  that  our  Lord  condemn-
ed polygamy  and  extrajudicial  divorce,  he  ex-
pressly declared,  that  fornication  (a  general  term
for  unchastity)  did  dissolve  the  marriage  con-
tract, and  that  a  man  was  justified  in  this  case,
(as  indeed  reason  and  experience  testify)  in  put-
ting away  his  wife.  The  exception,  therefore,
which  Christ  expressly  makes  to  the  indissolubi-
lity of  marriage,  is  a  proof  that  it  is  not  a  sacra-
ment however  convenient  it  may  be  to  popish
policy  to  make  it  such.

Q.  Does  the  Roman  Catholic  Church  forbid
marriage,  and  recommend  the  disuse  of  it,  decla-
ring celibacy  and  virginity  to  be  higher  states  of
perfection  than  the  holy  estate  of  matrimony  ?

A.  The  Romanists  teach,  that  though  at  the
beginning  God  commanded  marriage,  yet  now,
that  the  world  is  peopled,  the  command  is  not
binding  on  all.  On  the  contrary,  they  say,  that
both  Christ  and  St.  Paul  recommended  celibacy
and  virginity  as  a  higher  state  of  perfection.
Celibacy  is  recommended  to  all,  as  a  high  attain-
ment, but  to  the  priests  it  is  absolutely  enjoined.


227

Marriage  in  their  case  is  the  greatest  sin.  Virgins
too  are  taught  to  believe  that  they  live  in  a  more
holy  state,  and  that,  while  married  women  cannot
be  saved  without  a  purification  in  purgatory,  a
virgin  and  a  nun  will,  after  death,  pass  immedi-
ately into  paradise.

Q.  Are  these  interdictions  and  injunctions  con-
trary to  scriptvu'e  ?

A.  Yes,  there  is  no  command  in  scripture  to
forbid  the  marriage  of  any  whether  clergy  or
laity,  men  or  women.  In  the  New  Testament
marriage  is  declared  to  be  honorable  in  all,  and
the  younger  women  are  advised  to  marry ;  and
though  under  certain  circumstances,  (as  those
under  which  the  Christians  were  placed  to  whom
St.  Paul  wrote)  marriage  may  be  imprudence,
yet  the  Apostle  no  where  forbids  marriage.  On
the  contrary  he  teaches  us  to  regard  such  prohi-
bitions and  the  practices  that  have  accompanied
them,  as  the  decided  marks  and  proofs  of  the
heretical  apostacy  of  the  visible  Church.

Q.  Where  does  St.  Paul  teach  us  to  consider
these  practices  in  that  light  *?

A.  Particularly  in  the  I  Tim.  iv.  1,  2.  "Now
the  Spirit  speaketh  expressly,  that  in  the  latter
times  some  shall  depart  from  the  faith,  giving
heed  to  seducing  spirits  and  doctrines  of  devils,
speaking  lies  in  hypocrisy,  having  their  con-
science seared  with  a  hot  iron,  forbidding  to
marry  and  commanding  to  abstain  from  meats."

Q  2


2^8

Here  the  Apostle  tells  us  that  the  spirit  speaks
not  obscurely  but  expressly,  the  truth  word  for
word.  The  prophesy  is  remarkable,  and  so  is
the  fulfilment.  The  apostacy  here  spoken  of
is  under  the  visible  Church,  and  it  consisted  in
giving  heed  to  eroneous  doctrines  particularly  the
doctrines  of  demons  or  soul- worship,  which  was  the
popular  religion  of  the  gentiles,  and  which  St. Paul
frequently  condemns.  Two  kinds  of  demons  were
worshipped  by  the  gentiles,  those  who  never  dwelt
in  bodies  answering  to  angels,  and  those  had  lived
on  earth  or  canonized  mortals.  These  spirits  were
supposed  to  be  the  intercessors  and  agents  be-
tween gods  and  men,  and  to  carry,  from  earth  to
heaven,  and  from  heaven  to  earth,  prayers,  sup-
plications, rewaros,  and  gifts.  They  were  wor-
shipped in  images,  relicks,  and  shrines,  for  the
temples  of  the  heathen  were  the  sepulchers  of
dead  men,  in  which  they  were  worshipped  as
present.  This  idolatrous  theology  of  demons  or
soul-worship  has  been  for  many  centuries  prac-
tised in  the  Roman  Church  in  the  worshipping
of  saints,  angels,  and  the  Virgin  Mary ;  in
acknowledging  them  as  mediators  and  interces-
sors, in  addressing  prayers  to  them  and  bowing
down  to  their  images ;  and  in  worshipping  other
objects  such  as  the  host,  crosses,  relicks,  and
shrines.  This  species  of  idolatry  is  not  only  in-
consistent with  the  simplicity  and  purity  of  the
christian  religion,  and  opens  the  door  to  gross


229

superstition  but,  above  all  other  errors  and  acci-
dental heresies,  determines  the  manifest  charac-
teristics of  the  <^reat  apostacy  which  the  spirit
by  St.  Paul  foretold.  This  is  still  more  evident
if  we  consider  the  manner  and  persons  by  whom
it  has  been  introduced  and  supported,  viz.  by
those  who  forbad  marriage  and  commanded  to
abstain  from  meats.  It  was  monkery  with  its
vows,  and  trade  in  images,  relicks  and  lying  mi-
racles that  introduced  and  propagated  the  gentile
idolatry  under  christian  forms  and  sanctions.  And
though  both  charity  and  the  language  of  St.  Paul
induce  us  to  consider  the  apostacy  heretical  and
not  total,  yet  the  forbidding  to  marry,  and  the
interdicting  the  use  of  meats  and  regular  food,  as
maintained  by  the  Church  of  Rome  in  all  the
monastic  professions,  (of  which  these  are  the
essential  characteristics,)  as  well  as  with  respect
to  all  the  regular  and  secular  clergy,  nuns  and
consecrated  virgins,  are  plain  and  decided
marks  of  the  departure  of  that  Church  from  the
simplicity  and  purity  of  the  christian  faith  and
worship.

Q.  Has  not  experience  proved  that  the  ex-
treme doctrines  of  the  Church  of  Rome,  are
detrimental  to  morals  and  public  virtue  ?

A.  Common  sense  would  iead  us  to  expect
mischievous  results.  The  sacramental  doctrine,
the  dependance  of  marriage  on  intention  and  its
indissolubility,  connected  with  the  forced  celibacy

Q  3


230

of  the  clergy  and  monastic  orders,  have  propaga-
ted adultery  in  Roman  Catholic  countries.  The
indissolubility  of  marriage  is  so  evidently  a
policy  in  favor  of  those  who  have  no  wives,  and
who  have  every  advantage  for  making  use  of
those  of  other  men,  that  we  cannot  be  surprised
to  find,  that  adultery  is  prevalent  in  Roman
Catholic  countries,  and  especially  in  the  holy
city  itself.  Such  laws  are  so  evidently  opposed
to  nature  and  reason,  that  it  is  no  wonder  they
should  be  worse  than  vain.  The  appearance  of
hardship  provokes  resistance,  and  sin  taking
occasion  by  the  commandment  becomes  exceed-
ing sinful.  The  statutes  and  canons  in  force
prior  to  the  reformation,  and  the  lawful  marriage
of  the  clergy,  clearly  shew  that  concubinage
was  connived  at.  It  was  provided  that  priests
should  not  keep  their  concubines  puhllcly  in  their
houses,  nor  elsewhere  with  scandal^  nor  bequeath
property  to  them ;  that  none  should  let  houses
to  priests  that  kept  concubines,  and  that  a  severer
punishment  should  be  inflicted  for  the  sin  of
marriao;e  than  concubinasre.  Laws  are  not  made
for  righteous  men,  but  for  the  lawless  and  disobe-
dient, and  if  we  consider  that  great  facilities
existed,  by  monastic  institutions,  for  vicious  in-
dulgences, we  must  conclude  that  the  whole
system  of  the  Church  of  Rome,  w  ith  respect  to
marriages,  contravenes  morality,  as  it  does  na-
ture, reason,  and  scripture.    While  we  condemn


231

a  system  which  has  often  rendered  marriages  in
christian  countries  what  they  were  in  Rome  in
the  time  of  Tacitus  and  Suetonius,  a  "concubitus
promiscuus,"  we  must  be  far  from  judging  in-
dividuals in  the  Roman  CathoKc  priesthood,
amongst  whom  there  have  been  and  are  chari-
table men,  confidential  friends,  and  excellent
christians.

Q.  Have  some  countries  gone  into  a  contrary
extreme,  and  declared  marriage  to  be  a  civil  con-
tract without  any  reference  to  religion  *?

A.  When  the  errors  and  abuses  of  the  papacy
were  exposed,  those  evils  which  arose  out  of  the
doctrine  of  the  Church  respecting  marriage,  its
indissolubility  to  the  laity  and  its  interdiction
to  the  clergy,  attracted  particular  attention.  To
avoid  these  evils  some  had  recourse  to  the
remedy  of  declaring  marriage  a  civil  contract
and  nothing  more.  Abstracted  from  the  decla-
ration of  the  Jewish  and  Christian  scriptures,
and  indeed  from  the  sacramental  doctrine  of  the
Church  of  Rome,  marriage  may  be  regarded  as
a  civil  contract,  which  undoubtedly  it  is.  In
many  countries  where  marriage  could  only  be
solemnized  as  a  sacrament,  the  enacting  it  to  be
a  civil  contract  was  a  manifest  relief.  It  may,
however  be  questioned  whether  this  extreme  has
not  been  detrimental  to  morals.  In  France  the
system  has  been  adopted,  but  it  does  not  appear
to   have   contributed    to   a    very  high  degree  of


232

morality  or  social  happiness  in  that  country.
Some  public  documents  *  would  lead  us  to  con-
clude that  when  matrimonial  en2:aa:ements  are
guaranteed  only  by  civil  acts,  little  regard  is  paid
to  the  confirmation  of  the  contract,  or  the  faith-
ful observation  of  it  when  contracted.  In  Paris,
(if  we  take  into  the  account  intrigue  and  adultery)
promiscuous  intercouse  must  be  dreadfully  pre-
valent. In  such  habits  adultery  must  be  a  venial
gallantry,  which  husbands  and  wives  may  or  must
overlook.  The  French,  who  are  alive  to  the
sense  of  honour,  and  are  great  duellists,  seldom
retaliate  a  wrong  of  this  sort,   but   pass  it   over

It  must  be  remembered,  that  this  is  not  a  criterion  of  fornication
in  Stews  but  of  promiscuous  concubinage  in  the  place  of  mar-
riao'e,  and  also  that  infanticide  and  other  crimes  and  vices  not  to
be  named  are  extremely  common.


233

with  the  greatest  composure,  a  tact  which  might
be  illustrated  by  many  well  known  stories.  The
absence  of  religious  sanctions,  at  least  such  as
are  reasonable  and  practicable,  and  the  substitu-
tion of  those  which  are  unreasonable  and  imprac-
ticable, seem  to  conduct  to  the  same  immoral
results.  Under  such  a  system  of  morality,  where
the  legitimacy  of  children  born  in  wedlock  must
be  extremely  doubtful,  we  cannot  be  surprised  to
find,  a  corresponding  disregard  of  the  law  of  suc-
cession, hereditary  rights  and  the  reciprocal
duties  of  parents  and  children  ;  that  the  disre-
spect of  the  young  to  their  superiors  should  be
notorious,  and  that  the  law  should  find  it  neces-
sary to  protect  children  from  the  partiality  and
jealousy  of  their  parents.  It  must  be  confessed
that  a  certain  description  of  fornication  and  con-
cubinage has  increased  in  England,  but  this  must
be  ascribed  to  a  mischievous  law  which  contra-
venes, as  applied,  all  reason  and  decency.  Adul-
tery, however,  is  extremely  rare  among  the
peasantry,  and  when  once  marriage  is  contracted,
or  as  it  is  usually  expressed,  the  parties  "go  to
Church,"  fidelity  is  the  consequence,  and  that  in
cases  where  it  could  be  scarcely  expected.  A
fair  comparison  of  domestic  virtue  in  England
and  France,  would  lead  us  to  conclude  that  reli-
gion gives  the  greatest  firmness  to  morals,  and
that  without  it,  other  defences  to  virtue,  are
weak  and  untenable.     Where  the  law  proclaims


234

marriage  to  be  a  civil  contract,  and  provides  no
religious  sanctions  or  solemnization,  the  inference
with  vulgar  minds  is  direct  and  certain,  that  the
agreement  of  the  sexes  alone  is  sufficient  to  con-
stitute a  legitimate  union.

Q.  In  what  light  has  marriage  been  considered
by  theologians,  moralists,  and  statesmen  in  Eng-
land ?

A.  It  has  been  considered,  that  marriage,
though  not  a  sacrament,  is  in  part  a  religious  and
in  part  a  civil  contract.  Our  civil  law  considers
(and  perhaps  can  consider)  marriage  in  no  other
light  than  a  civil  contract,  and  the  law  regarding
marriage  as  it  does  all  other  contracts,  must  treat
unlawful  marriage  and  breaches  of  marriage,  not
as  sins,  but  civil  inconveniences.  The  law,  how-
ever, considers  no  marriage  as  lawful,  even
where  the  parties  are  willing  and  able  to  contract,
and  actually  do  contract,  unless  it  be  confirmed
by  the  religious  vows  and  solemn  forms  which
the  law  prescribes.  Our  legislators  and  states-
men seem  to  have  been  aware,  that  this  is  one  of
those  things  in  which  "  you  cannot  dishonour  the
sanctity  of  religion,  without  disordering  the  har-
mony of  civil  life"  for  this  is  precisely  one  of
those  cases  in  which  religion  is,  not  only  the  orna-
ment, but  the  support  of  civil  society.  The  law
of  the  land,  therefore,  has  sanctioned  the  reli-
gious solemnization  of  matrimony ;  and  the  due
publication  of  marriage,   the  religious  and  deco-


235

rous  espousals,  and  the  solemnization  before
God  and  the  Church,  have  contributed  to  pre-
serve the  domestic  virtue  and  confidential  inter-
course, which  characterize  the  people  of  England.
We  should  suppose,  that  this  is  a  system,  against
which  few  plaintiffs,  on  mature  deliberation,  could
be  found.  Ceremonies  are  of  great  consequence
and  legislators  and  governors,  and  honest  peo-
ple too,  ought  to  be  well  aware  what  rites  and
ceremonies,  employed  in  the  solemnization  of
marriage,  receive  the  legislative  authority.  If  it
should  be  allowed  for  any  number  of  individuals
to  form  immoral  associations,  and  the  solemniza-
tion of  marriage  become  one  of  the  social  acts
which  licentious  and  ignorant  men  may  attempt
to  regulate  ;  if  for  instance  a  St.  Simonian  sect,
should  open  the  door  to  anew  species  of  political
fanaticism,  which  may  at  the  same  time  let  loose
the  most  violent  passions,  we  have  not  even  a
correctional  police  to  restrain  them.  In  fact,  it
is  absurd  to  suppose,  that  the  system  of  registra-
tion, which  prevails  from  necessity  in  France,
could  be  applied  to  the  habits  of  English  society,
without  the  greatest  offence  to  the  feelings  of  the
people,  as  well  as  hazard  to  their  morals.  Licen-
tious rites  and  ceremonies  would  more  than  any
other  instruments  of  mischief,  undermine  and
sweep  away  those  mounds  and  sluices,  which  our
fathers  have  erected  against  the  tide  of  lawless,
immoral,  and  indecent  practices.


236

Q.  Did  Moses  provide  a  religious  ceremony
for  marriages  among"  the  Jews  ?

A.  Moses  did  not  provide  any  religious  ritual
or  ceremonies  for  the  celebration  of  marriage,
and  this  circumstance  is  certainly  worthy  of  ob-
servation. We  are  not,  however,  hastily  to  infer
that  he  was  indifferent  to  it,  either  as  a  legislator
or  a  moralist.  It  may  be  worth  while  to  examine
this  point.  Moses  certainly  did  not  institute  any
formality  of  a  religious  nature  for  the  celebration
of  marriage,  and  it  is  probable,  that  the  priest  or
minister  of  religion  was  not  particularly  concerned
in  the  consecration  of  the  marriage.  It  is  also
true,  that  in  all  civilized  societies  there  must  be
some  fixed  form  by  which  marriage  is  authorized,
and  by  which  it  may  be  precisely  distinguished
from  concubinage.  The  difficulty  is,  therefore,
that  Moses  did  not  provide  any  religious  or  any
civil  form  of  celebration.  How  is  this  omission
to  be  accounted  for  *?  If  Moses  had  instituted
any  form  of  celebration  of  marriage,  we  must
infer  from  the  analogy  of  his  whole  system,  that
it  would  have  been  a  religious  one.  He  was  not
indifferent  to  the  importance  of  marriage,  as  the
keystone  of  every  social  system ;  he  guarded
chastity  in  women,  before  marriage,  during  their
espousals,  and  after  marriage,  by  the  strictest
laws,  severe  guarantees,  and  the  most  solemn
religious  sanctions  which  were  accompanied  with
religious  ceremonies,  and  oaths  administered  by


237

the  priests  in  the  temple.  Moses,  therefore,  did
not  consider  marriage  merely  a  civil  contract,
but  one  that  was  to  be  recognized  by  appeals
(the  most  solemn  of  any  to  be  found  in  his  system)
to  conscience  and  to  heaven.  Why  then  did  he
omit  the  consecration  of  religion  (applied  to  so
many  affairs  of  infinitely  less  moment  and  applied
in  other  respects  to  marriage  itself)  in  the  so-
lemnization of  marriage  ?  There  appears  to  be
only  one  reasonable  explanation  for  this  omission,
and  a  clue  to  it  we  have  in  the  reason,  which  our
Lord  has  assigned,  for  Moses'  connivance  at  poly-
gamy and  divorce.  He  says  it  was  because  of  the
hardness  of  their  hearts.  The  Jews  certainly  disco-
vered, under  his  government,  attested  as  it  was,
a  rebellious  spirit,  and  we  may  well  suppose  that,
as  polygamy  and  divorce  were  common  in  all  the
world  at  that  time,  and  as  the  Jews  who  came
out  of  Egypt  lived  in  the  practice  of  them  to  a
very  great  extent,  (as  is  evident  from  the  census
of  the  people,  and  the  number  of  the  first-born)
it  would  have  been  a  moral  impossibility  to  have
annihilated  those  practices.  As  a  moralist,  how-
ever, and  as  the  inspired  historian  of  the  creation,
he  must  have  known,  that  those  practices  were
contrary  to  the  divine  institution  of  marriage.
As  a  civil  magistrate,  and  as  a  philanthropist,  he
was  obliged  (as  many  governors  are)  to  tolerate
evils  which  he  could  not  remove  without  convul-
sion and  inhumanity.       As   a  moralist,   and  a


238

divine  lawgiver,  he  could  not,  and  he  did  not,
apply  the  sanctions  of  religion  to  the  solemniza-
tion of  marriage,  because  those  sanctions  would
have  authorised  and  perpetuated  what  was  only
from  necessity  permitted — would  have  given  the
highest  moral  sanction  to  immoral  contracts,  and
dissolutions  of  contracts.  There  were  consuetudi-
nary ceremonies  for  the  celebration  of  marriage,
which  Moses  left  in  force.  It  is  probable  also,
from  the  distinctions  drawn  between  wives  and
concubines,  that  in  lawful  marriage  there  was  a
known  solemnity.  We  may  fairly  suppose  that
religion  was  introduced,  by  the  father  of  the  fam-
ily in  some  partriarchal  form,  and  that  these
customs  were  on  the  side  of  virtue  and  morality.
Those  cases  in  which  the  morality  was  clear,  as
seduction  and  the  proofs  of  chastity,  he  did  autho-
rize and  regulate.  Where  vice  was  to  be  tried
and  convicted,  he  appointed  oaths  and  religious
formularies,  but  where  under  a  system  of  divorce
and  polygamy,  he  could  not  have  enacted  oaths
and  religious  sanctions  for  the  marriage  of  one
wife,  which  was  a  moral  act,  without  at  the
same  sanctionino*  the  marriao;e  of  more  than  one
wife,  which  w'as  an  immoral  act  and  contrary  to
the  design  of  the  Creator,  he  applied  no  religious
ritual  of  the  altar,  but  provided  by  other  indirect
laws  to  secure,  as  much  as  possible,  the  virtue  of
society,  and  to  restrain  those  customs  and  habits
which   endangered  both   virtue   and  happiness.


239

The  Mosaic  system,  therefore,  when  thoroughly
examined,  comes  out  as  peculiar,  but  each  pecu-
liarity shews  the  divine  legation  of  TVloses.  This
omission  is  a  strong  argument  against  polygamy
and  divorce,  from  which  the  highest  moral  sanc-
tions were  withheld,  and  vindicates  the  morality
ot  the  character  and  system  of  Moses.  It  not
only  leaves  the  general  arguments,  which  have
prevailed  with  wise  and  good  men,  for  the  appli-
cation of  the  most  solemn  sanctions  to  marriage,
unimpaired,  but  we  think  also  further  confirmed
by  the  strongest  facts  and  implications.

Q.  Did  Jesus  Christ  provide  any  religious
ritual  for  marriage  ?

A.  It  was  not  to  be  expected  that  he  would
provide  a  religious  ritual  for  marriage  or  any
other  service.  Moses  was  a  civil  lea;islator  and
magistrate  for  one  nation,  and  therefore,  it  was
to  have  been  expected  that  he  would  have  ap-
plied the  sanctions  of  religion  (which  he  applied
to  less  important  contracts)  to  the  consummation
of  the  most  important  of  all  civil  contracts.  He
approached  this  point  of  legislation,  by  a  great
variety  of  laws,  but  we  have  seen  why  he  stopped
short,  and  did  not  apply  a  precise  religious  sanc-
tion to  this  contract,  in  a  state  of  society,  where
polygamy  prevailed.  Jesus  Christ  did  not  ap-
pear as  the  civil  lawgiver  or  king  of  the  Jews,
but  as  the  Saviour,  Prophet  and  Moralist  of  all
nations.    In  this  last  character  Christ  prescribed


240

a  moral  law,  and  not  a  civil  contract,  but  a
moral  law  that  was  to  effect  the  greatest  change
in  that  contract.  Christ  did  that  as  a  moralist,
which  Moses  as  a  civil  magistrate,  and  which
(speaking  after  the  manner  of  men)  Christ  him-
self as  a  civil  legislator  could  not  have  done.
Had  Christ  enacted  laws  in  detail  for  the  polity
of  the  christian  Church,  they  never  could  have
been  carried  into  effect  over  heathen  nations^
without  a  miraculous  conquest  of  the  world.
Moses  was  opposed  by  the  obduracy  of  one  nation
that  generally  acknowledged  his  divine  mission,
but  Christianity  was  to  be  spread  over  the  world  by
the  servants  of  Christ,  and  what  the  moral  state  of
the  world  was,  under  the  Roman  emperors,  is  well
known.  Christianity  for  its  morality  and  ministry
was  persecuted  with  a  vengeance  that  is  unparal-
leled in  the  history  of  mankind ;  and  what  must
have  been  the  consequence  if  Christ  had  made
positive  laws,  for  instance,  edicts  for  the  celebra-
tion of  marriage  and  the  suppression  of  polygamy
and  divorce  ?  If  the  virtuous  Pliny  or  the  generous
Trajan  had  discovered  such  edicts,  what  must
have  been  the  consequence  ?  What  must  have
been  the  fate  of  such  positive  laws  in  a  state  of
society,  where  marriage  was  neglected  to  such  a
decree  that  there  was  an  aversion  to  the  female
sex,  and  where  infamous  vices  (which  always
prevail  in  proportion  as  marriage  is  lightly
regarded  )  demoralized  the  people  to  the  lowest


241

degree,   and  depopulated    the    country  ?      The
Apostles  were   perfectly  aware  of  the   state   of
morals  among  the  heathen,  and  especially  among
the   enlightened    Romans   under   the  emperors.
St.  Paul  has    given,    in    his  first   chapter  of  his
epistle  to  the    Romans,   a   masterly    description
(restrained  as  it   was   by  a  sense  of  delicacy  )  of
the  infamous  vices  that  prevailed.     He  has  also
given  us   an    account,  in  his  first  epistle  to  the
Corinthians,  of  the  discreet  and  decided  manner
in  which  the  Christian  marriage  law  was  enforced
on  Christians,    and  on  Christians  only.     In  the
case  of  the  incestuous   marriage  in  the  church  of
Corinth  there  were  two  parties,  the  man  a  chris-
tian and  the  woman  a  heathen.     In  the  case  of
adultery,   Moses   applied  the   law  of  purgation
which  Michael  is  justly  considers  one  of  the  strong-
est proofs  of  his  divine  legation,  and  in  the  case
of  the  incestuous  marriage,  St.  Paul,    by  an  ap-
peal, in  the  name  of  Christ,  to   a   special  Provi-
dence (the  strongest  proof  of  the  divine  mission
of  Christ)  applied   a   similar    discipline    to   the
Corinthian  fornication,  but  of  the  heathen  woman
he  expressly  says,  "  What  have  I  to  do  to  judge
them  that  are  without  !<?"     Christ,  therefore,  and
his  apostles  applied  the  principles  of  the  christian
morality    to    Christians  and  not  to  the  heathen.
Moses  was  a  civil  le»:islator  and  inao:istrate,   and
therefore  did    not   enact  an   impracticable   law,
but  Christ  and   his   Apostles  confining  christian

R


242

morality  to  christians  and  enforcing  marriage  not
as  a  civil  but  as  a  religious  cojit7'act,  were  ena-
bled to  uphold  the  true  principles  of  morality  in
the  midst  of  the  greatest  vices.  It  is  religion,
therefore,  and  the  christian  religion  alone  that  is
the  support  of  christian  marriage.  It  is  the  chris-
tian religion  that  ameliorates  men's  hearts  and
renders  christian  laws  practicable.  The  wonder  is,
that  Christianity  by  the  force  of  its  morals,  effect-
ed the  regeneration,  which  it  did  effect  in  this  par-
ticular, among  the  christian  converts  at  the  first,
and  on  the  conversion  of  the  empire,  among
whole  nations.  The  force  of  christian  morals
effected  in  whole  nations  the  law  of  monogamy,
without  the  permission  of  extrajudicial  divorce,
in  defiance  of  the  strongest  passions  and  the  most
vicious  habits  and  practices  of  mankind.  Christ,
therefore,  did  not  enact  a  civil  contract,  but  revi-
ved the  moral  law  that  has  effected,  what  neither
heathen  legislators  nor  Moses  himself  could
effect,  the  recognition  of  a  pure  moral  principle
by  civil  law,  or  the  civil  contract  of  marriage  on
divine  and  christian  principles.  It  is,  therefore,
both  absurd  and  ungenerous,  to  speak  of  christian
marriage  as  a  mere  civil  contract.  Polygamy
and  extrajudicial  divorce,  or  what  is  almost  the
same  thing,  promiscuous  concubinage,  never  were
and  never  will  be  restrained  without  the  sanc-
tions of  Christianity.  One  husband  and  one  wife
is  the  peculiarity  of  Christian  countries,  and  it  is


243

an  excellency  and  blessing  which  we  owe  not  to
the  wisdom  of  legislators  and  civilians,  (who  are
too  much  disposed  to  lean  to  the  hardness  of
men's  hearts)  but  to  the  influence  of  the  christian
religion.  If  that  influence  be  scouted,  and  a
change  be  effected  in  the  sentiments  and  feelings
of  the  community,  we  must  expect  a  hardness
of  heart,  and  a  corruption  of  practice,  (not  con-
fined to  the  higher  orders)  that  will  defy  legisla-
tors and  civilians,  though  they  were  as  wise  as
Moses.  Let  us  hope,  however,  that  truth  and
reflection  will  have  their  due  weight,  and  that
no  such  change  will  be  predestinated  in  the  mo-
ral feelings  of  our  country,  a  change  which  every
honest  man  and  virtuous  woman  must  sincerely
deprecate.  The  law  of  the  creation,  the  morali-
ty of  the  gospel,  and  the  law  of  the  land  are  now
in  perfect  harmony.  Whatever  errors  there  may
be  in  men's  opinions  or  practices,  the  religious
celebration  of  marriage,  as  a  vow  and  covenant
before  God  and  the  christian  church,  does  not
imply  the  application  of  solemn  sanctions  to  im-
moral principles.  On  the  contrary,  religious
solemnization  prevents  error,  negligence  and
clandestine  marriage,  and  strengthens  conscience
and  principle  in  the  contracting  parties.*  When

*  Though  we  differ  with  Roman  Catholics,  respecting  their
doctrines  and  regulations  on  this  subject,  we  agree  with  them  in
their  religious  sentiment,  that  marriage  ought  to  be  a  contract  of
the  altar.  This  is,  in  fact,  not  merely  the  sentiment  of  Christianity,

the  civil  contract  has  been  tried,  it  has  tended  to
demoralize,  as  in  France  where  the  christian
Church  is  degraded,  and  during  the  usurpation
in  England,  when  marriages  were  allowed  to  be
contracted  before  a  justice  of  the  peace,  for  no
other  reason  (as  Dr.  Paley  observes)  but  to  de-
grade the  clergy.  Whatever  was  the  motive
immorality  was  the  consequence — an  immorality,
which  nothing  but  the  virtuous  principles  inculca-
ted by  the  clergy  (commonly  called  the  moral  or
latitudinarian  writers)  under  God,  restrained.
There  is  nothing  more  liable  to  absurdity  and
abuse  than  religious  ceremonies  applied  to  mar-
riage. It  is  evident,  that  the  greatest  mischiefs
must  arise  from  abstract  legislation  applied  to
tenets,  opinions,  ceremonies,  and  ministrations
unknown.  As  laws  cannot  quadrate  with  the
wishes  or  interests  of  all,  reasonable  and  well  dis-

but  of  all  men  when  they  express  their  best  and  strongest  feelings.

Dum  queror,  et  divos,  quanquam  nil  testibus  illis
Profeci,  extrema  moriens  tamen  alloquor  hora.

The  vow  and  covenant  in  Christian  marriage  is,  however,
a  peculiar  protection  to  females,  as  might  be  illustrated  in  a  great
number  of  examples.  When  a  man  treats  his  wife  with  injury,
he  is  commonly  reminded  of  his  vow  to  protect.  At  the  last
county  assizes  for  Norfolk,  there  was  a  case  of  this  kind,  and  the
learned  judge  in  endeavouring  to  convince  the  criminal  of  his  sin,
expressed  a  sentiment  which  was  in  accordance  with  the  feelings
of  every  christian  present ;  "The  object  upon  whom  your  malicious
and  abominable  design  was  intended,  was  her  whom  you  had  led
to  the  altar  of  God  and  there  vowed  to  protect  and  support."


245

posed  christians,  will,  on  reflection,  be  content
with  those  consuetudinary  practices,  which  have
obtained  a  wholesome  controul  over  the  senti-
ments and  feelings  of  a  christian  country.  There
may  be  theoretical  difficulties,  and  practical  in-
conveniences, but  they  will  yield  to  great  and
common  advantages.  This  is  certainly  one  of
the  most  delicate  subjects  of  human  policy,  and
that  on  which,  it  is  the  fundamental  duty  of  the
legislature  to  legislate  only  for  the  morals  of  the
country.  Gratitude  and  truth  demand  the  ac-
knowledgment that  christian  marriage  is  partly
a  religious  and  partly  a  civil  contract,  and  that
Christianity  has  made  the  contract  what  it  now  is.
Sound  policy  must  avow,  that  the  best  means  of
preserving  christian  marriage,  as  it  now  exists,
is  by  the  application  of  religious  sanctions,  and
that  when  religion  and  law  combine  to  conse-
crate that  contract,  which  is  the  fountain  head  of
the  relations  of  life,  marriage  is  most  likely  to  be
both  morally  and  legally  observed,  as  it  has
generally  been  in  this  country  to  the  happiness
and  honour  of  the  community.
